Michael Saylor & Phong Le: What Could Actually Break Strategy?74 min42 insights2026-11-20
▶ Watch on YouTube
Investor Q&A in which Michael Saylor and Phong Le argue Strategy is a treasury company ('central bank of the crypto economy') that creates MSTR value by issuing digital credit like STRC against ~$40B of net reserves at a ~10.5% hurdle rate. They defend buying back STRC below $100 amid a six-month credit contraction (expecting organic demand of $5-7.5B at 12%), cite 3.4 years of USD cash coverage, say only Bitcoin going to zero is existential, and pitch a future of tokenized credit, bank stablecoins and AI agents demanding yield.
Capital structure and what each Strategy security is · 5How Strategy creates shareholder value: the treasury-company spread model · 8Metrics: mNAV thresholds, gross vs net Bitcoin per share, amplification · 8STRC defense: buybacks near par and the credit-market contraction · 11Downside resilience and existential risk · 5Long-term vision: digital credit, digital money, stablecoins and AI agents · 5
Capital structure and what each Strategy security is 5
Strategy has roughly four security classes: ~$6.5B of six senior 144A convertible bonds (held mostly by convert arbs), short-duration digital credit STRC, long-duration digital credit (STRF, STRD, STRE, STRK), and MSTR common stock.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategySTRCSTRFSTRDSTRESTRKMSTR
So what: Map of seniority and investor fit across the Strategy stack; retail mostly cannot access the converts.
“There's about 6 and a half billion of them.” ▶ 3:54
Saylor positions STRC for investors with a 4-month to 4-year horizon who want high yield without Bitcoin volatility, while STRF/STRD/STRE/STRK serve 4-10+ year investors who want a fixed, locked-in dividend (including a euro-denominated option).
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highSTRCSTRFSTRDSTRESTRK
So what: Product segmentation by duration is central to how Strategy sells credit.
“So stretch STRC is short duration digital credit.” ▶ 4:36
Per Phong Le, Strategy's convertible and cumulative convertible debt notional is about $6.7B against ~$4.9B of USD assets (net ~$1.8B), with ~$12.7B of preferred stock below it, ~$52.5B of Bitcoin reserves and ~$36B of equity value.
data_point🗣 Phong Leconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Balance sheet snapshot: preferreds sit behind converts, both heavily covered by BTC reserves.
“Uh notional is about $6.7 billion and we have about US dollar assets of about $4.9 billion.” ▶ 9:17
Saylor says MSTR offers three return sources: a Bitcoin proxy (Strategy owns about 4% of all Bitcoin that will ever exist), amplified Bitcoin at roughly a 1.3-1.5x factor via credit issuance, and ownership of the 'central bank of the crypto economy' that earns the spread between credit cost and Bitcoin returns.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highMSTRBitcoinStrategy
So what: Equity thesis hinges increasingly on the digital-credit franchise, not just BTC beta.
“we own 4% of all the Bitcoin there's ever going to be in the world.” ▶ 6:52
Phong Le says Strategy holds over 850,000 Bitcoin and has the most liquid preferred securities in the world, and that this scale makes its credit issuance more effective.
data_point🗣 Phong Leconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Scale and liquidity are framed as the competitive moat for treasury-company credit.
How Strategy creates shareholder value: the treasury-company spread model 8
Saylor frames Strategy as a treasury company analogous to an insurer or bank: it issues digital credit against roughly $40B of net reserves and captures the spread between the credit's cost (~10% hurdle) and Bitcoin's appreciation, with residual gains accruing to common shareholders.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategyBitcoinJP Morgan
So what: MSTR value creation is a function of BTC return minus hurdle rate times credit outstanding.
“A treasury company creates uh shareholder value by creating digital credit.” ▶ 6:31
Saylor says Strategy could issue $4B of credit against ~$40B of net reserves and be 10x over-collateralized, and in general can create credit roughly equal to its reserves.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumStrategy
So what: Implied credit capacity ceiling on the order of net reserves.
“We could issue $4 billion of credit against the 40 billion in net reserves and it would be 10x over collateralized when we did it.” ▶ 10:24
If Bitcoin appreciates only at the ~10% hurdle rate, Saylor concedes credit issuance is roughly value-neutral for MSTR holders, and if Bitcoin returns 0%, gross leverage becomes dilutive.
causal🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highBitcoinMSTR
So what: The bull case for MSTR's leverage requires BTC to beat ~10.5% annually; skeptics should see it as negative.
“But if Bitcoin appreciates 10% a year and the hurdle rate is 10%, then the issuance of the credit looks sort of neutral.” ▶ 11:28
Saylor puts Strategy's hurdle rate at about 10.5% on roughly $20B of gross Bitcoin funded by ~$6.5B of convertibles plus ~$12B of digital credit; at 30% annual BTC returns that is a ~20% spread on $20B over a decade.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Quantifies the leverage payoff under a Bitcoin-maxi scenario.
“the hurdle rate, which is about 10 and a half% for us” ▶ 27:21
Saylor says a liquid credit instrument lets Strategy sell ~$250M in a day and create $250M-$500M of value in hours, and that if Bitcoin compounds ~20%+ a year, each $1B of credit sold creates roughly $2B of shareholder value, implying $20-40B of value creation in an expanding credit market.
prediction🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumStrategySTRCBitcoin
So what: Upside for MSTR is concentrated in windows when credit markets reopen.
“when you sell a billion dollars of credit, you probably created $2 billion of shareholder value.” ▶ 1:03:36
Saylor cites the 2024 $21B equity shelf, fully issued in about four months during a bullish equity market, as capturing roughly $14B of shareholder value, and says a similar ~$21B shelf now exists for STRC.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategySTRC
So what: Precedent for rapid issuance once demand turns; STRC shelf is ready to deploy.
“we actually issued all 21 billion dollars in like four months and we captured about uh $14 billion of shareholder value in four months” ▶ 1:02:53
Saylor argues that without a working credit business Strategy would be a mere holding company valued at roughly a 0-20% premium to NAV for optionality; credit success is what could scale it to a $100B, $1T, then $10T company.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategy
So what: mNAV premium is explicitly tied to credibility of the digital-credit franchise.
“If the credit works, we can sell 10 billion, then a hundred billion, then a trillion” ▶ 51:19
Saylor claims it is the credit investors, not equity or capital investors, that legitimize a treasury company; equity investors then award a premium if they believe in the credit business.
contrarian🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategy
So what: Watch STRC demand and pricing as the leading indicator for MSTR's premium.
“It's the credit investors that legitimize a treasury company.” ▶ 1:06:46
Metrics: mNAV thresholds, gross vs net Bitcoin per share, amplification 8
Saylor lays out swap accretion thresholds: issuing MSTR for dollars is net-Bitcoin accretive above 1.0x mNAV, swapping MSTR for Bitcoin is accretive above roughly 1.29-1.3x mNAV, and at 1.5-2x+ the company can generate immediate Bitcoin yield, having done tens of billions of such transactions.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highMSTRBitcoinStrategy
So what: Concrete mNAV levels that dictate whether ATM equity issuance is accretive.
“It's like about 1.3 right now or 1.29.” ▶ 15:06
Even below 1.0x mNAV, swapping MSTR for STRC can be accretive if the equity discount is shallower than STRC's discount to par (e.g., mNAV 0.98 vs STRC at $95); if MSTR falls below that threshold, Strategy can instead swap Bitcoin for STRC.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highMSTRSTRCBitcoin
So what: Strategy has accretive levers even when MSTR trades below NAV, including selling BTC to retire STRC.
Net Bitcoin per share is the liquidation-value metric for short-horizon pessimists, while gross Bitcoin per share matters to long-horizon bulls; Saylor says relying on either alone is a mistake and the right choice depends on time horizon, Bitcoin outlook and hurdle rate.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Investors should model their own BTC return assumption versus ~10.5% cost to pick the relevant metric.
“I think that's why it's a mistake to think that um the only thing that matters in net Bitcoin per share” ▶ 29:33
Phong Le explains that gross Bitcoin per share assumes all convertibles convert and ignores BTC price, whereas net Bitcoin per share converts USD liabilities to BTC at the current price and treats out-of-the-money converts as debt, making it the precise metric used for day-to-day swap decisions.
framework🗣 Phong Leconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Net BTC/share is the operational decision rule; it falls when BTC price drops.
“net Bitcoin per share is impacted by Bitcoin price where gross Bitcoin per share is not impacted by Bitcoin price.” ▶ 35:36
Phong Le says Strategy targets doubling gross Bitcoin per share over a 7-year period.
prediction🗣 Phong Leconviction: mediumStrategyBitcoin
So what: Long-term KPI target investors can track.
“we say we're going to double our gross Bitcoin per share over a 7-year period.” ▶ 34:56
Saylor's rule of thumb: a transaction is immediately accretive to common holders if net Bitcoin per share rises, whereas gross Bitcoin per share can be inflated by any borrowing (e.g., a 20% overnight margin loan vs 2% 100-year bonds) so its quality depends on credit terms.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Scrutinize the cost and duration behind any gross BTC/share gains.
“a very simple rule of thumb is it's immediately accretive if there's a net Bitcoin per share accretion.” ▶ 38:28
Saylor argues amplification is dynamic, not static: Strategy abandoned convertible bonds because it could not sell enough to maintain ~1.3-1.5x amplification at scale, moved to perpetual digital credit like STRK, then concluded short-duration STRC was most scalable; STRC traded about 100x more than some long-term perpetuals in a day.
causal🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategySTRCSTRK
So what: Durability of amplification, not current leverage, should drive MSTR valuation.
“like yesterday STRC traded a hundred times more in the market than some of the long-term perpetuals.” ▶ 32:53
Saylor says no single metric can value Strategy; it requires a nonlinear dynamic simulation (his MIT thesis topic) that cannot be done on a spreadsheet because of phase shifts.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumStrategyMIT
So what: Signals management views simple mNAV/BTC-per-share heuristics as insufficient.
“you can't do it on a spreadsheet.” ▶ 34:17
STRC defense: buybacks near par and the credit-market contraction 11
Saylor views STRC as investment-grade digital credit: about 5x over-collateralized, with internal models implying a fair credit spread of 60-70bp (vs a 150bp investment-grade threshold), yet paying a 12% dividend versus ~6% for typical IG credit, and roughly 2-3x better after tax-deferred return-of-capital treatment.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highSTRCStrategy
So what: Management will buy STRC below $100 because it views the yield as far above fair for the risk.
“Our credit models, you know, spit out a fair credit spread of 60 or 70 basis points.” ▶ 40:47
Saylor's stance on STRC trading below par: Strategy will buy it back rather than let it trade cheap, likening it to Apple refusing to discount the iPhone.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highSTRCApple
So what: Implicit soft floor near $100 backed by company repurchases.
“if you don't think it's worth $100, sell it, right?” ▶ 41:55
Saylor says Strategy recently raised $8-10B of capital to support STRC and estimates STRC trades at roughly a 500bp premium to its risk.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumSTRCStrategy
So what: Large commitment of balance sheet to defending the flagship credit.
“We we just, you know, raised 8 to10 billion dollars in capital to support it.” ▶ 41:05
Saylor diagnoses a broad credit contraction since roughly May: long-end sovereign yields up 100-150bp, IG spreads up 70-90bp, CCC rates up ~400bp, neocloud bonds moving from ~9% to 15% yields, private credit seizing up, and smaller issuers crowded out by hyperscalers like Alphabet and SpaceX issuing debt.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highAlphabetSpaceXprivate credit
So what: STRC weakness is framed as part of a macro credit squeeze, especially AI/neocloud credit.
“Triple C rates have increased 400 basis points.” ▶ 43:23
Saylor says the crypto winter has lasted about 14 months since Bitcoin's all-time high last October, while the credit-market contraction has run about six months.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumBitcoin
So what: Two overlapping downturns explain current pressure on Strategy's securities.
“So we've been in a crypto winner for 14 months” ▶ 42:49
Saylor says demand for STRC fell from about $10.5B at a 10-11% yield six months ago to roughly $7B at 12%, and predicts natural organic demand at a 12% dividend settles between $5B and $7.5B.
prediction🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumSTRC
So what: Expect STRC outstanding to shrink further before it stabilizes at par.
“somewhere between five billion and seven and a half billion dollars uh there is natural organic demand” ▶ 46:13
Saylor describes STRC management as a continuous Dutch-auction process: buy back when demand is weak until supply and demand balance at $100, then reissue when credit markets expand; he even floated a formal tender offer at $100, which would take about a month and a proxy.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumSTRCStrategy
So what: A possible tender offer is an optionality catalyst for STRC holders.
“It would it would take like a a proxy statement and it would take like a month, but we could say we're ready to buy back at a hundred.” ▶ 44:58
Saylor refuses to raise the STRC dividend to 15-20% to boost demand because a higher rate raises the hurdle rate and risk to equity holders; he says 20-30% annual growth for a ~$70B company is sufficient.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highSTRCStrategy
So what: Signals dividend rate discipline; not chasing growth via yield.
“If I if if our business model works, we'll be growing 20 to 30% a year.” ▶ 49:35
Phong Le says Strategy IPO'd STRC about a year ago at $2.5B, sold about $8B at $100 while credit markets were open, then over the last four to five months bought back about $3B at $95-99 to de-risk as credit tightened.
data_point🗣 Phong Leconviction: highSTRCStrategy
So what: Shows procyclical amplification: issue at par in good markets, retire below par in bad.
“We took uh we bought back $3 billion of stretch at less than what we issued it for.” ▶ 55:36
Saylor argues that how Strategy treats credit holders in a bear market defines its brand for a decade, comparing a failure to stand behind STRC to an insurer refusing hurricane claims or an iPhone catching fire; he accepts unprofitable short-term decisions for 1-7 year reputational payoffs.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategySTRCApple
So what: Management prioritizes credit quality over near-term shareholder accretion.
“People judge you based upon how you treat them in difficult markets in the bare markets” ▶ 0:19
Saylor lays out the failure mode: overselling credit causes duration and volatility to rise, holders sell, buyers vanish, confidence in the credit collapses, and confidence in the equity follows.
causal🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategySTRC
So what: STRC breaking par is a key risk signal for MSTR holders.
“And if we sell too much of it, the credit will collapse.” ▶ 52:27
Downside resilience and existential risk 5
Strategy has about 3.4 years of USD duration (cash to cover preferred dividends and interest) and could run without touching capital markets for three years; Saylor says its balance sheet has never been better and credit is about 40% backed by dollars.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategy
So what: Reduces near-term forced-BTC-selling risk in a prolonged downturn.
“Right now, we're about 40% backed by dollars.” ▶ 1:00:01
Saylor says the only existential risk to Strategy's model is Bitcoin going immediately and irrevocably to zero; in a prolonged bear market it can always monetize volatility or arbitrage credit against equity instruments to de-risk.
thesis🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: highStrategyBitcoin
So what: Management dismisses prolonged-bear liquidation scenarios.
“the only situation I think that really represents existential risk to us with our business model is Bitcoin has to go immediately irrevocably to zero tomorrow” ▶ 58:57
Saylor contrasts Strategy's daily, continuous financing and risk adjustment with low-frequency 20th-century financing like annual 144A bond deals or LBO debt that hangs over a company for eight years.
contrarian🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumStrategy
So what: Frames Strategy's ATM-driven model as structurally more adaptive than traditional leverage.
“Our financing is we decide how much credit or equity to issue every day.” ▶ 58:13
Phong Le notes that the last time Bitcoin was at $62,000 Strategy had only 0.7 years of USD duration and STRC fell to $75-80, whereas now it has 3.4 years and STRC is around $95 and likely strengthening.
data_point🗣 Phong Leconviction: highBitcoinSTRCStrategy
So what: Cash reserve build-up materially improved STRC resilience versus the prior drawdown.
“Last time Bitcoin was at $62,000, we had 0.7 years of USD duration and stretch price down to $80, $75.” ▶ 1:00:12
Saylor says STRC traded about $600M in a single day, more than every income-bearing preferred stock in the US capital markets this year, calling it likely the most liquid preferred income instrument in the world.
data_point🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumSTRC
So what: Liquidity is the key enabler for rapid issuance when demand returns.
“STRC uh traded $600 million yesterday.” ▶ 1:01:29
Long-term vision: digital credit, digital money, stablecoins and AI agents 5
Saylor's STRC objectives: a 3-4 month duration, volatility below 10, a 10-12% dividend, liquidity of $500M to $1B a day, and AUM growing from ~$7-8B toward $100B, which he says alone could make Strategy a trillion-dollar company.
prediction🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumSTRCStrategy
So what: Milestones (vol <10, daily volume, AUM) investors can track to judge progress.
“Getting the volatility below 10 and keeping it there is a big deal.” ▶ 1:08:41
Saylor envisions a later 'digital money' product: a one- to two-day instrument bought and sold at par yielding 7-8% when the risk-free rate is about 4%; combined with STRC this could capture ~1% of the credit market and support a multi-trillion-dollar enterprise value.
prediction🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: exploratoryStrategySTRC
So what: Signals a possible money-market-style product as the next leg of the franchise.
“we can go and grab 1% of the credit market and we can sell hundreds of billions of dollars of credit.” ▶ 1:10:30
Phong Le predicts that within three years every major bank will issue its own stablecoin, all securities will trade tokenized 24/7, and AI agents will have banks and conduct commerce.
prediction🗣 Phong Leconviction: mediumstable coinagents
So what: Tokenization and agentic finance are seen as the distribution channel for digital credit.
“In three years, every major bank in the world will have a stable coin issued by that bank.” ▶ 1:11:03
Phong Le argues AI agents will unemotionally demand stablecoin yields of 6-7%+ rather than ~3.5% Treasury yields, and that blending 12% STRC with Treasuries could back ~6% stablecoin yields, positioning STRC and Bitcoin as underlying collateral for future DeFi.
thesis🗣 Phong Leconviction: exploratorySTRCstable coinDeFiBitcoin
So what: Second-order bet: STRC as a yield component inside stablecoins and tokenized products.
“you mix that with a treasury and you might be able to provide 6% yields on a stable coin as an example.” ▶ 1:12:06
Saylor argues credit-market success arrives suddenly: large allocators who watch an issuer for years under stress eventually 'flip a switch', moving $500M in 30 seconds and potentially $10B in a month, so the shelf, programs and USD reserves must be in place beforehand.
framework🗣 Michael Saylorconviction: mediumStrategySTRC
So what: Demand for STRC may inflect abruptly; preparedness is the reason for current defensive spending.
“When they flip that switch, $500 million moves in 30 seconds.” ▶ 0:51
Why Bitcoin Could Be Setting Up for a Massive Bull Market44 min24 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Bo Turner, CEO of show partner Abundant Mines, argues the public-miner exodus to AI is lowering difficulty and benefiting remaining Bitcoin miners, that ASICs are hitting physical limits so rigs last longer, and that low-cost hydro/stranded energy plus 100% bonus depreciation keep mining viable at ~$80K BTC ahead of the next halving. Partly promotional partner content.
Miner pivot to AI and its effect on hashprice · 7Energy costs, grid demand and stranded power · 5ASIC hardware hitting physical limits · 2Mining economics, halving and taxes · 7Politics, price path and adoption · 3
Miner pivot to AI and its effect on hashprice 7
Bitcoin miners with large energy capacity are pivoting to AI/HPC because the same megawatt can generate roughly 5-10x more revenue, a pull especially strong for public miners judged on quarterly earnings.
causal🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highAIBitcoin mining
So what: Explains why large public miners are converting capacity; power access is the scarce asset.
“makes a lot of sense to pivot towards something that's going to make you five maybe 10 times as much on a revenue basis with the same megawatt” ▶ 1:28
Because miners exiting to AI are reducing network competition, miners that remain are earning more Bitcoin per unit of compute than a year ago, a rare reversal, and Turner expects mining to keep getting easier absent a major bull market.
prediction🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumBitcoinhash rate
So what: Remaining miners gain share of block rewards; falling difficulty is a tailwind for pure-play miners.
“the fact that it's actually gotten easier and in fact I think it will continue to get easier absent a major bull market” ▶ 2:48
Capacity converted to AI is unlikely to return to Bitcoin mining because AI conversion costs 10 to 50 times as much as the original buildout, making the hashrate exit sticky.
causal🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumAIBitcoin mining
So what: Difficulty relief from AI conversions is structural rather than cyclical.
“after you've spent 10, sometimes 50 times as much on getting that capacity ready for AI” ▶ 3:21
Much of the announced public-miner exit has not yet hit the network: companies like Marathon are still running capacity until their AI facilities come online, so network difficulty may fall considerably further.
prediction🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumMarathonCore Scientific
So what: Expect further difficulty declines as announced AI conversions complete.
“We may see it go down quite a bit further based on those big players coming offline.” ▶ 38:38
Over a 5-10 year horizon the miner landscape will look very different, shifting away from large public companies toward distributed, small-scale, stranded-energy operators positioned further upstream near generation; Core Scientific was cited as exiting mining entirely.
prediction🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highCore Scientificstranded energy
So what: Hashrate composition shifts from public megasites toward small upstream operators.
“you will see the makeup of miners on the network shift more toward uh distributed more toward I think smallcale stranded energy” ▶ 37:09
Turner warns that miners who 'overnight' rebrand as AI companies chasing current money risk shiny-object syndrome and may regret it in a few years; Abundant Mines treats each site as a highest-and-best-use real estate decision and has kept all facilities on Bitcoin mining so far, with a couple of sites likely first to convert.
contrarian🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumAbundant MinesAI
So what: Skepticism on the durability of opportunistic AI pivots by miners.
“anybody who's just piled into it without really thinking about the long-term effects of where your cash flows are coming from, you know, we'll see how that plays out in a few years” ▶ 6:25
Hydropower sites are especially attractive for AI conversion because they offer stable, high-uptime, non-intermittent power without needing nearby battery capacity.
data_point🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumhydropowerAI
So what: Hydro-backed mining sites carry AI optionality value.
“It's super high uptime even without redundancy. So, it's a great fit for those conversions.” ▶ 18:32
Energy costs, grid demand and stranded power 5
Depending on estimates, 10-30% of the US national grid could be requested for AI capacity over the next decade, unplanned demand that will push up energy costs on top of general inflation in labor, equipment and maintenance.
data_point🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumAInational grid
So what: Rising power costs pressure on-grid miners; behind-the-meter supply becomes an edge.
“anywhere from 10 to 30% of the national grid could be asked for to be used for AI capacity over the next decade or so” ▶ 4:17
Bitcoin mining can act as an interim offtaker for new energy projects (e.g. solar) waiting 3-7 years for grid interconnection, giving them a return on capital and de-risking completion.
framework🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumBitcoin miningsolar
So what: Mining as bridge financing for new generation is a durable niche as easy sites disappear.
“what's holding you up is an interconnection which means giving it a pathway to actually go to market where people can buy it. And that can take anywhere from 3 to seven years.” ▶ 16:50
Mining on vented landfill-gas methane (which he describes as about 20 times worse than CO2) can make the Bitcoin network carbon negative in aggregate.
contrarian🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumlandfill gasmethane
So what: ESG counter-narrative for mining relative to AI's energy use.
“there's actually a way where mining turns the entire Bitcoin network carbon negative” ▶ 15:29
Abundant Mines' Oregon operations run primarily on Columbia River hydropower, a network of about 23 dams supplying about 85% of the region's power, and hydro assets can last 70-80 years versus 10-15 for solar panels.
data_point🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumAbundant MinesColumbia RiverOregon
So what: Long-duration, low-cost hydro underpins a structural cost advantage.
Turner rejects the premise that using more energy is bad, arguing civilizational progress and poverty reduction have come from channeling more energy, not less.
thesis🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highenergy
So what: Frames Bitcoin and AI energy use as productive rather than wasteful.
“the more energy that we use actually the better off people are” ▶ 13:09
ASIC hardware hitting physical limits 2
Bitcoin ASICs are no longer becoming obsolete within a year: chip fabrication capacity is being absorbed by better-capitalized AI companies, and chips are near the physical limit (bleeding edge ~3nm, some working on 2nm, down from 130nm), so it may take half a decade before a miner twice as efficient appears, versus a 10x leap from 2016 to 2020.
thesis🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highASICAIBitcoin mining
So what: Longer useful life of mining rigs improves depreciation economics and lowers capex churn.
“you might have half a decade before there's something that's twice as efficient as what's out today” ▶ 21:19
AI GPUs cannot economically threaten Bitcoin: mining with GPUs loses money, and redirecting AI chips to attack the network would be far less efficient and have nowhere near the compute of existing ASICs.
causal🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highGPUNvidiaASIC
So what: AI chip boom poses no security risk to Bitcoin's hashrate.
“You you can mine Bitcoin with a GPU, but it's a money losing proposition.”
Mining economics, halving and taxes 7
With Bitcoin around $80K and a production cost of about $50K, miners earn a structural 30-40% gross margin regardless of price swings, which is why Abundant Mines roughly doubled its business during the bear market.
data_point🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highBitcoinAbundant Mines
So what: Mining is viable at current prices for low-cost operators.
“If Bitcoin's at 80K and my cost of production is 50” ▶ 11:20
The 'One Big Beautiful Bill' restored 100% bonus depreciation, so a miner buying $100,000 of equipment can deduct the full amount in year one against ordinary income, alongside electricity and professional fees, making mining a flexible, scalable tax offset similar to real estate cost segregation.
framework🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highOne Big Beautiful Billbonus depreciation
So what: Tax treatment drives a meaningful share of retail mining demand, especially at year end.
“In the first year, because of the big beautiful bill, you can depreciate that equipment 100%.” ▶ 26:06
Turner cautions that mining as a tax strategy only makes sense if you already want Bitcoin; pursuing tax benefits through a nonsensical investment is not sound wealth advice.
framework🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highBitcoin
So what: Tax benefits should not be the sole reason to mine.
“the point of this ultimately is that it's an investment you would actually want” ▶ 27:25
A single hosted machine currently generates a few hundred dollars a month at current economics.
data_point🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumASIC
So what: Benchmark for small-scale mining revenue.
With the next halving roughly a year and a half away, miners will need Bitcoin to roughly double to keep today's mining economics; otherwise survival depends on good equipment, operational excellence and structurally cheap energy.
prediction🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highhalvingblock subsidy
So what: Halving squeezes high-cost miners; a price doubling is needed to maintain margins.
“it's going to be a a hard proposition without Bitcoin going up, you know, double from here so that you keep the same mining economics” ▶ 35:52
As mining grows more competitive, Turner does not expect any company to again build a roughly gigawatt-scale on-grid facility in one place solely to mine Bitcoin, as Riot did.
prediction🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumRiot
So what: Large greenfield on-grid mining builds are over; growth moves to stranded or niche energy.
“I don't think you'll see probably any company develop like the Riot facility that's about a gigawatt in one place on grid ever again to mine Bitcoin” ▶ 34:51
Mining only makes sense if you are bullish on Bitcoin's USD price, because it gets progressively harder to earn the same amount of Bitcoin over time.
thesis🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: highBitcoin
So what: Mining is a leveraged bet on price appreciation.
“mining Bitcoin makes sense pretty much only if you are bullish on Bitcoin” ▶ 35:09
Politics, price path and adoption 3
Turner expects a 'blue wave' political shift to make it increasingly difficult to add new large mining capacity, which would advantage operators with already permitted, running sites.
prediction🗣 Bo Turner (CEO, Abundant Mines)conviction: mediumblue wave
So what: Regulatory headwinds raise the value of existing permitted capacity.

Brunell notes Bitcoin has fallen from last October's all-time high to trade between roughly $70K and $80K, and is surprised it sits in the 70s nearly five years after 2021's $69K high despite expecting it to exceed $200K; she believes a very big bull market is due.

The Lawfare Podcast 2 episode(s) · 60 insights

Rational Security: The “Alfred Hitchcock’s The Bird Dogs” Edition83 min37 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Lawfare's Rational Security panel argues a week of US outages (FAA ground stop, Microsoft, Chase and other banks, NSA network) reflects AI-accelerated vulnerability patching colliding with Cold War-era infrastructure and depleted federal staff rather than attacks, with cascade risk centered on communications; it dismisses DOJ threats to prosecute Democratic overseers as performative stalling, and debates whether the Nvidia CEO's resignation over an affair with a suspected Chinese spy tied to chip diversion (Mega Speed) will tip a Bessent-vs-Lutnick administration split toward tighter China export controls.
Infrastructure outage cluster: AI-driven patching meets aging systems · 8Cascade failure and wartime exposure · 3Policy response: funding, resilience campaigns and why reform stalls · 9Executive branch vs. incoming Democratic Congress oversight · 6Nvidia CEO scandal and the export-control split · 11
Infrastructure outage cluster: AI-driven patching meets aging systems 8
A cluster of US outages in one week — an FAA nationwide ground stop from an air traffic control communications failure, a global Microsoft outage taking down Outlook and Teams, Chase and other big banks' login/payment failures, and the NSA's classified network going dark — were officially attributed to mundane causes (a misbehaving router, a latent software bug, a routine patch gone wrong), not cyberattacks.
data_point🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: highFAAMicrosoftOutlookTeamsChaseNSA
So what: Correlated operational failures across aviation, cloud software, banking and intelligence show systemic fragility even without an adversary.
“a misbehaving router, a latent software bug, a routine patch gone wrong, are decidedly mundane” ▶ 3:24
Former counterintelligence infrastructure-protection official Mike Feinberg argues the outages stem from the collision of ancient US critical infrastructure (last concertedly modernized during the Cold War) with AI models that surface vulnerabilities at unprecedented speed, forcing many organizations to patch roughly simultaneously and creating widespread friction.
causal🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highAIcritical infrastructure
So what: Expect more frequent benign-cause disruptions as AI-accelerated vulnerability discovery drives mass simultaneous patching on legacy systems.
“It's a combination of aging technology and aging systems meeting new technology and new systems that can point out weak spots we previously didn't know about.” ▶ 8:53
Feinberg's operating rule from government counterintelligence work: never assume bad actors where mere incompetence suffices as an explanation; he urges the public to calm down and not panic about malevolent actors over the past week's outages.
framework🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highcounterintelligence
So what: Default to operational-failure explanations for outage clusters absent evidence of attack; avoid panic-driven positioning.
“never assume bad actors where mere incompetence will suffice as an explanation” ▶ 7:03
Host Scott Anderson argues AI has made cyber risk more systemic: AI can identify waves of vulnerabilities quickly, and organizations racing to fix them lean on AI-driven automated coding tools whose trial-and-error nature can degrade product reliability when moving fast.
causal🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: mediumAI
So what: AI-assisted remediation itself becomes a source of outages; reliability risk rises for software-dependent services.
Molly Roberts notes AI cuts both ways: it also helps bad actors exploit vulnerabilities, which creates the urgency to patch, and that patching can cause unintended downstream breakage — so vulnerability exists both as exploitation risk and as remediation risk.
framework🗣 Molly Roberts (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: mediumAI
So what: The defender's patch race is itself a fragility channel, not just the attacker threat.
“the vulnerabilities exist on two levels” ▶ 12:18
Feinberg contends aging critical infrastructure is itself a national security threat: if systems can be brought down by patching a vulnerability, they are not sophisticated enough to repel an attacker.
thesis🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highcritical infrastructure
So what: Benign outages are a signal of weak attack resilience, raising the case for infrastructure security spending.
“aging critical infrastructure, even if it seems relatively innocuous, is in and of itself a national security threat” ▶ 13:03
Feinberg says both government and the private sector habitually prioritize user interface and front-end experience over back-end security, and private infrastructure owners as profit-maximizers underinvest because security is expensive.
causal🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: mediumcritical infrastructure
So what: Structural underinvestment in back-end security persists until regulation or liability changes incentives.
Feinberg blames an unprecedented lack of human capital in the US government on the administration giving Elon Musk veto power over agency headcount, which produced RIFs and a toxic atmosphere driving voluntary exits — a self-inflicted weakening of infrastructure security capacity.
causal🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highElon MuskUS government
So what: Reduced federal cyber/infrastructure staffing raises the probability and duration of future disruptions.
“We have an unprecedented lack of human capital in the US government which is a problem entirely of our own making.” ▶ 16:01
Cascade failure and wartime exposure 3
Feinberg highlights cascade failure across DHS's 16 designated critical infrastructure sectors: information and communications technology is the keystone, since payments/financial networks and SCADA-controlled utilities (water works, power) cannot function if communications go down.
framework🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highDHSSCADA
So what: ICT/telecom outages are the highest-impact tail risk because they propagate into finance and utilities.
“there are 16 sectors of critical infrastructure as designated by DHS” ▶ 16:33
Feinberg says the US was lucky the week's major failure was in transportation, which has relatively few cascade effects on other sectors in peacetime.
thesis🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: mediumFAA
So what: Transportation-only disruption is contained; the same event in ICT would be far more damaging.
Feinberg warns that in wartime the Department of Defense and intelligence community rely heavily on civilian infrastructure such as commercial flights and civilian logistics, so an FAA shutdown during a WWII-type mobilization would seriously hinder US force projection.
contrarian🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highDepartment of DefenseFAA
So what: Civil aviation and logistics resilience is a defense readiness issue, supporting dual-use infrastructure funding arguments.
“if this FAA shutdown occurred during an era like World War II, we'd really be screwed” ▶ 18:47
Policy response: funding, resilience campaigns and why reform stalls 9
The UK government's 'Be Ready' campaign asks households to prepare for 72 hours of disruption (no transport, cash machines or groceries; stockpile clean water) citing extreme weather and cyber/ransomware events, with Anderson suggesting the implicit motivation is anticipated AI-era volatility.
data_point🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: mediumUKBe Ready
So what: Governments are beginning to price in more frequent multi-day disruptions and shift preparedness onto households.
Anna Bower and the panel doubt a US equivalent of the UK's household-preparedness campaign would succeed, citing COVID-era resistance to federal directives and urban space constraints, while UK reaction has itself been mixed with citizens asking what the government isn't telling them.
thesis🗣 Anna Bower (Lawfare editor)conviction: mediumUKCOVID
So what: Government preparedness messaging can trigger suspicion and panic buying rather than orderly preparation.
Feinberg reports that every emergency food supplier in the US is currently sold out due to public overreaction to the outages, and expects restocking.
data_point🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: mediumemergency food supplier
So what: Outage clusters create short-term demand spikes for preparedness goods; likely transitory.
“Every emergency food supplier in the country right now is sold out” ▶ 25:30
The Trump administration blamed Congress for chronic underfunding and requested $30–50 billion to modernize air traffic and related systems, even though Congress had largely granted full FAA and Transportation Department budget requests; the accounting behind the figure has drawn scrutiny.
data_point🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: mediumTrump administrationCongressFAA
So what: A potential $30–50B surge in aviation infrastructure modernization spending is on the table, though its basis is contested.
“give us 30 to50 billion dollars so we can update this” ▶ 26:40
Anderson notes prior infrastructure efforts largely fizzled: 'infrastructure week' in the first Trump term produced little, and many IRA provisions addressing infrastructure were only partly implemented and then rescinded by the current Congress.
data_point🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: mediumIRACongress
So what: Track record suggests large comprehensive infrastructure packages are unlikely to materialize quickly.
Feinberg argues comprehensive critical-infrastructure reform may be politically impossible because the US system is a mixed public-private arrangement where government regulates, auctions spectrum and grants rights of way but doesn't own the assets, making coordination across 16 sectors extremely hard (citing Youngstown Sheet and Tube limits on seizing production).
thesis🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highYoungstown Sheet and Tubecritical infrastructure
So what: Expect piecemeal, slow remediation rather than a coordinated national upgrade.
“it might be impossible politically” ▶ 28:20
Feinberg says a large amount of US critical infrastructure equipment is built by companies in adversary nations — a ton of Chinese-built equipment and more Russian-built equipment than people realize — a legacy of building on lowest-cost inputs.
data_point🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highChinaRussia
So what: Rip-and-replace of adversary-made infrastructure gear is a large latent spending need for trusted domestic suppliers.
“I don't think people realize how much Russianbuilt equipment there is in US critical infrastructure.” ▶ 30:25
Feinberg explains forcing private owners to rip and replace insecure foreign equipment with US-made replacements means compelling billions in private spending and raises takings clause / eminent domain reimbursement questions, making it easy to punt indefinitely — which is what has happened.
causal🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: hightakings clause
So what: Rip-and-replace mandates face legal and fiscal friction; any program likely requires federal reimbursement funding.
“this is a problem from hell” ▶ 32:07
Anderson floats shifting liability onto private infrastructure operators that fail to update adequately as a policy lever, while noting it raises coordination and systemic problems.
framework🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: exploratoryprivate sector
So what: Liability regimes for infrastructure operators are a possible future regulatory risk.
Executive branch vs. incoming Democratic Congress oversight 6
Ahead of Democrats taking control of Congress in January, White House counsel Will Sharf warned incoming Democratic chairs their plans could cross into criminality and directed agencies to get White House sign-off before any official testifies; DOJ was reportedly encouraged to adopt a 'wartime posture'.
data_point🗣 Anna Bower (Lawfare editor)conviction: highWill SharfWhite HouseJustice DepartmentCongress
So what: Expect maximal executive-branch resistance to oversight: privilege claims, prolonged subpoena battles.
“blatant criminality” ▶ 3:56
Anna Bower calls the letters from US attorney Aaron Wrights (Southern District of Texas) to Democratic lawmakers legally baseless: they claim statements like 'lawyer up' implicate Klan Act conspiracy-against-rights statutes, but the speech or debate clause protects legislative acts and he lacks evident authority outside his district.
thesis🗣 Anna Bower (Lawfare editor)conviction: highSouthern District of Texasspeech or debate clause
So what: Criminal threats against legislators are political theater, not a credible legal threat.
Feinberg, who attempted prosecutions under the conspiracy-against-rights statute, says DOJ rarely brings it, case law is poor, and its most prominent recent use was prosecuting Donald Trump over the 2020 election; he sees only two explanations for the letters — idiocy or performance for the base — neither a real threat.
thesis🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highJustice DepartmentDonald Trump
So what: Discount legal risk to congressional oversight; the real tool is delay.
“idiocy is generally not a good legal theory” ▶ 42:29
Molly Roberts argues the real effect of prosecution talk is deterrence of potential non-administration witnesses who might otherwise answer subpoenas, since contempt referrals would go to Attorney General Todd Blanch, who presumably wouldn't prosecute.
causal🗣 Molly Roberts (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: mediumTodd Blanch
So what: Oversight may yield less testimony than expected because witnesses face intimidation without enforcement backstop.
Feinberg expects the administration's main strategy to be refusing cooperation and running out the clock — the tactic used during the 116th Congress and the Jack Smith inquiries — including asserting executive privilege to force litigation and exploiting bad-faith novel arguments that need briefing through appeals.
prediction🗣 Mike Feinberg (Lawfare senior editor)conviction: highJack Smithexecutive privilege
So what: Oversight investigations will be slow; meaningful results within the two-year term are uncertain.
“So, why deviate from a winning strategy?” ▶ 50:25
Anderson counters that stalling will work less well than in the first Trump term because key questions now have answers (DC Circuit on appropriations clause suits; Mazars confirming third-party subpoenas) and the Justice Department has 'deep-sixed' the presumption of regularity, making DC courts skeptical of government assertions.
contrarian🗣 Scott R. Anderson (host)conviction: mediumDC circuitMazarsJustice Department
So what: Courts may move oversight disputes faster than in 2019–2020, raising odds of disclosures.
“the justice department has completely deep six the presumption of regularity” ▶ 55:17

Nvidia's CEO (Jensen Huang, as rendered variously in the transcript) resigned after revelations of an affair with Alice Wang, CEO of MegaSpeed ('Mega Speed'), whom US officials suspect of spying and facilitating advanced AI chip diversion to China; he has since publicly signaled willingness to return as CEO if asked.

Anderson notes chip and AI stocks are down amid the scandal and that Marco Rubio had written a memo to Trump in the summer saying the woman was probably a spy, meaning cabinet officials knew for months.

Roberts suggests a broader turning point on AI safety may be coming: Democrats ran partly on AI-related issues with data centers figuring prominently, and the political mood toward AI may be turning.

Feinberg thinks an Nvidia CEO comeback is entirely possible and sees few real consequences for Nvidia or its CEO, because tech companies prioritize profit and access to the Chinese market and workforce over national security, feigning concern only where it wins contracts or avoids liability.

Roberts clarifies her view as a meaningful split inside the administration over how to win the AI race — profit-prioritizers vs. those wanting tighter advanced-chip controls — with the profit side previously winning and possibly not winning as clearly going forward.

Lawfare Daily: The Story of PEPFAR, with John Donnelly40 min23 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Journalist John Donnelly, author of 'Lifeline', explains how George W. Bush's $15B PEPFAR program (2003) scaled AIDS treatment in Africa via a State Department-run, target-and-accountability model, generated soft power (85% US favorability in Africa in 2008 vs under 40% now, below China), and created parallel systems whose dependency was exposed when DOGE shut down USAID in 2025.
Pre-PEPFAR treatment gap and why Bush acted · 6Execution model: State Department, targets, accountability · 8Key people and political conversions · 3Soft power and national security returns · 2Dependency, transition and the 2025 aid shock · 3Could the US do this again · 1
Pre-PEPFAR treatment gap and why Bush acted 6
In 2000, HIV was a manageable chronic disease in the US thanks to triple-combination antiretrovirals, but only about 30-35,000 Africans were on these drugs while another 5-6 million lacked access, making infection a death sentence.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highHIVAfricaantiretroviral drugs
So what: Illustrates how a large access gap for an existing proven technology can be the binding constraint, not innovation.
“if you were [clears throat] HIV positive in an African country in 2000, it was basically a death sentence for you.” ▶ 1:19
Condoleezza Rice persuaded Bush to act on African AIDS by bringing in CIA analysts and National Intelligence Council staff, with the CIA described as the US agency that cared most about the AIDS crisis in Africa.
anecdote🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highCondoleezza RiceCIANational Intelligence CouncilGeorge W. Bush
So what: Intelligence community framed pandemic disease as a security risk, a lever for funding global health.
“the agency that has cared the most about the AIDS crisis in Africa and that was the CIA” ▶ 6:40
Bush backed PEPFAR on two grounds: national security (neglected poor countries breed vulnerability to attacks like 9/11 and the 1998 Kenya and Tanzania embassy bombings) and his religious obligation to help the poor; Donnelly concluded the compassionate motive dominated over PR or security optics.
causal🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highGeorge W. BushPEPFAR9/11KenyaTanzania
So what: Large foreign-aid commitments can be driven by leader conviction rather than electoral calculus.
“for Bush, it was really the compassionate case to do something” ▶ 8:44
Donnelly argues PEPFAR was unlikely under an Al Gore presidency, framing it as a 'Nixon to China' dynamic where a Republican took the boldest step while Democrats pushed incrementally.
contrarian🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumAl GorePEPFARNixon
So what: Big policy shifts often require the side least expected to champion them.
“in some ways a Nixon to China parallel” ▶ 9:52
US global HIV spending rose from about $900 million a year at the end of the Clinton administration to a $500 million mother-to-child transmission initiative and then a $15 billion, five-year PEPFAR commitment announced in Bush's 2003 State of the Union with targets of treating 2 million people and preventing 7 million infections.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highClinton administrationGeorge W. BushPEPFARState of the Union
So what: Step-change, target-based funding rather than incremental increases defined the program.
“So Bush didn't just up it a little bit, he blew it wide open.” ▶ 10:57
Opposition to PEPFAR came from Karl Rove (no political benefit for the 2004 election and no Marshall Plan-style on-the-ground capacity, only a dozen or so embassy staff per country) and from OMB under Mitch Daniels, where analyst Robin Cleveland grilled Fauci and Dybul over the $15 billion cost.
framework🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highKarl RoveOMBMitch DanielsRobin ClevelandTony FauciMarshall Plan
So what: The core skeptic arguments were execution capacity and opportunity cost versus domestic spending.
“you're going to get no political benefit from launching this thing” ▶ 11:45
Execution model: State Department, targets, accountability 8
The Bush administration housed PEPFAR in the State Department rather than USAID because it did not fully trust USAID to execute and meet outcomes, putting ambassadors in 15 focus countries in charge of country plans.
causal🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highPEPFARState DepartmentUSAID
So what: Institutional placement outside the incumbent aid bureaucracy was seen as key to performance.
“they didn't fully trust the USA ID could actually execute the program” ▶ 13:41
In Kenya, US AIDS funding went from $25 million pre-PEPFAR to $100 million in year one, about $220 million in year two and $500 million within five years, swamping the rest of the embassy budget and pulling together USAID, CDC, DoD, Peace Corps and NIH staff.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highKenyaPEPFARUSAIDCDCPeace CorpsNIH
So what: Shows the scale and speed of the funding ramp in a single country.
Early PEPFAR rollout created a race among embassies to place antiretroviral drug orders because supply could not meet the sudden new demand.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumPEPFARantiretroviral drugs
So what: Demand shocks from large public procurement programs can create supply bottlenecks for drugmakers.
“There was a race um among the embassies about putting in your drug orders fast” ▶ 14:58
PEPFAR adoption spread via a 'Lazarus effect': three or four months after a clinic opened, recovered early patients brought 5-15 others to get tested, overcoming AIDS stigma once a positive test no longer meant death.
causal🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highPEPFARLazarus effect
So what: Visible treatment success is the most powerful driver of testing uptake and demand.
“It was it was the the the Lazarus effect”
PEPFAR built a large data collection system to measure performance against targets, redirecting extra effort to lagging countries.
framework🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highPEPFAR
So what: Measurement infrastructure was central to the program's effectiveness.
“they set up a huge data collection system” ▶ 16:27
Gary Edson at the NSC shaped a 'Bush doctrine' for foreign assistance: set clear objectives and targets, hold recipients accountable, and target countries with low corruption and the logistics to scale; this produced separate entities like the Millennium Challenge Corporation, PEPFAR and the President's Malaria Initiative.
framework🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highGary EdsonNSCMillennium Challenge CorporationPEPFARPresident's Malaria Initiative
So what: A replicable model for results-based aid: dedicated vehicles, targets, and performance-linked funding.
Donnelly attributes PEPFAR's success to the targeted model, performance-based funding (countries missing targets got less money, outperformers got more), and an inspiring 'mission of a lifetime' that made staff work exceptionally hard.
causal🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highPEPFAR
So what: Funding tied to measurable performance plus mission motivation explains outperformance relative to typical government programs.
“They responded to performance.” ▶ 27:47
Donnelly found PEPFAR's system empowered smart people to act quickly with ample money in the pipeline, likening coordinators to commanders on a war footing who shifted resources day to day.
framework🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumPEPFAR
So what: Adequate, flexible funding plus delegated authority enables rapid adaptation.
“it's almost like a a war footing in a way” ▶ 35:53
Key people and political conversions 3
Ugandan doctor Peter Mugyenyi built Africa's largest public AIDS treatment program (about 2,000 patients by 2002) and imported generic antiretrovirals without regulatory approval, invoking an emergency provision until the regulator allowed repeated imports.
anecdote🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumPeter MugeniUgandageneric drugs
So what: Local champions bending rules on generics paved the way for scale-up.
“people have to take these drugs every day, so I'm not going to do this one time” ▶ 19:05
Mark Dybul, Fauci's NIH deputy who drafted the AIDS plan and became PEPFAR ambassador in 2006, has recently been renamed to head the Global Fund and is pushing African governments to build their own health systems to absorb PEPFAR patients.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumMark DyelGlobal FundNIHTony FauciRandall Tobias
So what: Leadership at the Global Fund signals a push toward country ownership and transition of donor-funded HIV programs.
“just uh recently is been renamed to to head the global fund” ▶ 20:06
Bono converted Senate Foreign Relations chair Jesse Helms, who had called foreign aid 'throwing money down a rat hole' and stigmatized AIDS, by framing infants born HIV-positive through a biblical leper story; Helms then urged Dick Cheney that Bush should listen to Bono.
anecdote🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highBonoJesse HelmsDick CheneyGeorge W. BushSenate Foreign Relations Committee
So what: Moral framing aimed at the strongest opponent can unlock bipartisan support for large spending.
“foreign assistance was like throwing money down a rat hole” ▶ 22:23
Soft power and national security returns 2
Polling in 2008, PEPFAR's fifth year, showed 85% favorability of American leadership in Africa; it is now below 40%, and China's favorability in Africa is higher than that of the US today.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highPEPFARAfricaChina
So what: US soft power in Africa has eroded sharply, ceding ground to China.
“now it's actually below 40%. U China is higher in Africa than in its favorability today” ▶ 29:10
Donnelly says PEPFAR goodwill made it easier to establish the US military presence in Djibouti and helped a CIA station chief in Khartoum get what he needed, showing soft power opening doors for security interests.
causal🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumDjiboutiCIASudanPEPFAR
So what: Health aid translates into tangible strategic access and intelligence cooperation.
“the soft power can open many doors” ▶ 30:49
Dependency, transition and the 2025 aid shock 3
PEPFAR created parallel procurement, logistics and data systems instead of integrating with national health systems, and Donnelly argues it should have begun transitioning to countries after its first 10 years when it was no longer an emergency.
contrarian🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highPEPFAR
So what: Donor-built parallel systems create transition risk when funding is cut.
“it did it did that for far too long” ▶ 31:37
When DOGE shut down USAID over a few weeks in 2025 with a 90-day foreign assistance freeze, chaos followed, including a crisis because US-collected patient monitoring data had not been shared with national governments; data and funding were later worked out.
data_point🗣 John Donnellyconviction: highDOGEUSAIDPEPFAR
So what: Abrupt aid cuts expose operational dependencies such as data ownership.
“there was a crisis just on sharing data in the beginning” ▶ 32:42
Despite the abrupt US pullback, African HIV programs largely continued because PEPFAR had trained tens of thousands of doctors, nurses and lab technicians, creating a strong health professional cadre, and because the US government is still putting lots of money in.
thesis🗣 John Donnellyconviction: mediumPEPFARAfrica
So what: Human capital built by aid provides resilience even when health systems overall remain weak.
“There's a very strong health professional cadre in in most African countries” ▶ 33:11
Could the US do this again 1
Donnelly argues that just as US politics moved from Reagan refusing to say 'AIDS' to Bush launching a $15 billion AIDS program 20 years later, today's divisiveness could give way to renewed bipartisanship enabling large-objective programs.
prediction🗣 John Donnellyconviction: exploratoryRonald ReaganGeorge W. BushPEPFAR
So what: Political regimes shift; bipartisan big-program capacity may return.
“PER shows that the US government can tackle the biggest problems in the world” ▶ 39:30

US Home Prices Set To Fall Faster Than Even The Bears Expected | Melody Wright102 min50 insights2026-11-19

The New Harbor segment covers de-risking equities, a brutal precious-metals shakeout, and 5%+ Treasury yields.

National home prices turn negative; foreclosures drive acceleration 11
Redfin's October sales data showed year-over-year national home prices turning negative for the first time in three years, which Melody Wright calls a very significant corner.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highRedfinUS housing
So what: Signals the national housing correction has moved from forecast to measured reality.
“for the first time in three years we have year-over-year national negative home prices” ▶ 0:01
Melody Wright now expects US home price declines to accelerate faster than she had anticipated, potentially as soon as Q1 2027, if a stock market correction and a data center pullback compound rising foreclosures.
prediction🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highUS housingdata centers
So what: Shortens the timeline for her previously 'slow grind' bearish housing call.
“we could re really be looking at acceleration in Q1 faster than I would have anticipated” ▶ 0:25
Wright contrasts today with 2023: then, year-over-year negative prices were halted by SVB, the BTFP and Nvidia mania, and medians floated up because mostly higher-priced homes transacted; now insurance, property taxes and electricity costs are much higher and foreclosure sales are large enough to drag medians down.
causal🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highSVBBTFPNvidiaforeclosures
So what: The forces that stopped the 2023 decline are absent and new cost/forced-sale pressures are present.
Foreclosure sales are now making a dent in median prices because regular sales are pulling back more significantly than in 2023 as rates rise, while forced sales increase, reversing the mix effect that held medians up for years.
causal🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highforeclosuresmortgage rates
So what: Composition of sales is flipping from high-end-supported to distress-weighted, pushing reported medians lower.
“It's making a dent. It's and it's making a dent because here's what's happening. Rates are going up.” ▶ 15:01
The FHA cohort is a key source of accelerating foreclosures; restrictions imposed on FHA loss mitigation in October 2025 take this long to bite, and borrowers will exhaust their options by end of Q4 2026/Q1 2027.
causal🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highFHA
So what: FHA-heavy markets and lenders face rising forced sales into early 2027.
“those restrictions that went on the FHA last year in October 2025, it takes this long for that to make a difference” ▶ 33:09
Wright frames 2026 as the year of the motivated seller and 2027 as the beginning of the distressed seller.
framework🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highUS housing
So what: Expect more forced listings and price cuts through 2027.
“27 is going to be the beginning of the distress seller” ▶ 17:36
Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac prime portfolios started showing early delinquency in the summer; Wright expects it to show up clearly in the numbers in about 12 to 18 months despite remaining loss mitigation options.
prediction🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumFannie MaeFreddie Mac
So what: Distress is spreading from FHA/non-QM into prime agency credit.
Wright expects foreclosure sales to reach 2007 levels by around Q2 2027, at which point the national downturn will be undeniable.
prediction🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumforeclosures
So what: A visible inflection that could shift mainstream narrative and policy response by mid-2027.
“when we get to 2007 level of actual foreclosure sales, which I think we'll probably get there by Q2 of next year” ▶ 33:47
The Black Knight/ICE delinquency report for last month acknowledged delinquencies are back at 2019 levels, which commentators wrongly dismiss as a return to norms.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumBlack KnightICE
So what: The 'normalization' narrative masks an ongoing upward trend in delinquency.
“we are now back at 2019 levels” ▶ 33:38
Any foreclosure moratorium (the 2011-style 'regulatory playbook') would come too late to stop the decline, because many investors are already walking away and handing back keys to dozens of single-family homes regardless.
contrarian🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumforeclosure moratorium
So what: Don't count on policy to put a floor under prices.
“the horse has already left the barn” ▶ 16:03
Wright says the only thing that could arrest the national housing downturn would be large pay raises plus bringing missing workers back into the labor force.
thesis🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highreal wageslabor force
So what: Watch wage growth and labor participation as the key reversal signals.
“Unless we all get huge payraises and we bring those missing people from the workforce back in” ▶ 34:18
Data center boom towns: Texas permits, financing stress and fragile local markets 12
New officials elected in the recent blue wave with anti-data-center or pro-regulation agendas pose a significant threat to data center construction jobs and, by extension, boom-town housing markets that have offset national weakness.
thesis🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highdata centersTexas
So what: Data-center-driven local housing markets are a key weak link in the bull case.
Wright cites a Goldman Sachs study estimating 45% of data center projects could be impacted, with about 25% of that in Texas.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumGoldman SachsTexasdata centers
So what: Texas is the epicenter of regulatory risk to the AI buildout.
“Goldman Sachs did a study and said 45% of projects could be impacted” ▶ 2:25
Most Texas data center projects are 'behind the meter', building their own gas power plants, but many got started on minor air permits (the kind used for a dry cleaner) when they actually need major air permits, so permits are now in question and could be denied or delayed.
causal🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highTexasMetaBlackRockProject Jupiterair permits
So what: Permitting is an under-appreciated Achilles heel for the AI capex cycle; even delays hurt.
“they kind of cheated and got what's called a minor air permit to get started” ▶ 2:54
At Stargate Abilene the construction workforce peaked around 9,000 and is already rolling over, partly because Microsoft's planned expansion lacks a permit.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumStargateAbileneMicrosoft
So what: Early sign that data center construction employment has peaked locally.
Abilene, Texas (population ~130,000) has home prices up 26% year over year and median rent of $2,600 versus a US median of $1,400, showing how data center booms distort local housing.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highAbileneTexas
So what: Boom-town housing is highly exposed if construction crews leave.
“home prices are up 26% year-over-year in a town of 130,000 people” ▶ 4:22
On her 2,000-mile Texas data center road trip, Wright found boom-town excess (a $447 Courtyard Marriott night in Abilene, $13 food-truck lemonade at the Shackelford site) while hotels and restaurants elsewhere were empty, explaining how data center spending is propping up the economy.
anecdote🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumAbileneCourtyard Marriottdata centers
So what: Economic resilience is concentrated in data center towns and is fragile.
Data center developers are running out of money: mechanics liens are being filed against Vantage projects because contractors aren't paid fast enough, and Vantage faces a federal lawsuit in San Antonio over permitting.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highVantageSan Antoniomechanics liens
So what: Liquidity stress, not politics, is the bigger near-term threat to the buildout.
“all these people are running out of money” ▶ 7:08
Neocloud projects relying on private financing are stalling; Wright visited a Cloudburst site where construction had barely begun as private financing dries up.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumCloudburstneocloudsprivate credit
So what: Smaller AI infrastructure players are the most vulnerable to a credit pullback.
A short-term rental operator in Abilene saw occupancy fall about 20% from roughly 83% over several months, after Abilene had been the top small Airbnb market last year.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumAbileneAirbnb
So what: Boom-town demand is already fading quickly.
“They've seen over the last several months about a 20% drop in that occupancy” ▶ 19:50
Wright argues the debt markets, not politicians, will decide the data center buildout's fate, and that Oracle credit default swaps are not yet wide enough given how exposed Oracle is.
contrarian🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highOraclecredit default swaps
So what: Oracle credit looks mispriced relative to its data center exposure.
“those credit default swaps are not wide enough on Oracle yet cuz they are so exposed here” ▶ 20:37
Project Jupiter, the Oracle/OpenAI site on the Texas-New Mexico border, was denied a local pipeline, obtained expedited federal approval, and is now being challenged by the New Mexico attorney general, illustrating that even federal rescue still means delay.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumProject JupiterOracleOpenAINew Mexico
So what: Federal intervention can't fully prevent delays.
Wright expects the federal government may step in to buy land or save some data center projects, but sees significant delays regardless because many operators 'want out' as their balance sheets bleed and diesel costs rise.
prediction🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumfederal governmentdata centers
So what: Headwinds are more likely than a rescue-driven acceleration.
“I think there are people that want out, Adam. They want out because they are drowning.” ▶ 23:03
Credit stress seen from the mortgage servicing back end 6
At a Dallas mortgage servicing conference, servicers broadly confirmed Wright's bearish outlook and said loans the agencies sold to hedge funds are not reporting to credit, hiding true delinquency.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highmortgage servicershedge funds
So what: Official delinquency data likely understates actual distress.
“all of those sales out into that the agencies did off to the hedge funds are not reporting to credit” ▶ 27:20
Wright notes non-qualified (non-QM) private mortgages were about 12% delinquent last year, and servicer reactions suggested current conditions are worse than she thought.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumnon-QM mortgages
So what: Non-QM credit is a hot spot for losses.
REO vendors told Wright they processed as many real-estate-owned properties in the last two months as in the prior two years, alongside exponential increases in bankruptcies and skyrocketing short sales.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highREOshort salesbankruptcy
So what: Rate of change in bank-owned inventory is spiking—a leading indicator of price pressure.
“we did in the last two months, we did as many AROs as we've done in the last two years” ▶ 29:01
Wright, echoing Steve Eisman, argues the REIT, multifamily and Airbnb operators who came from Wall Street around 2016 don't understand credit, while seasoned back-end servicing staff do and are preparing for trouble.
framework🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumSteve EismanREITsmultifamily
So what: Look to downstream credit people, not originators/sell side, for early warning.
Wright expects the mortgage credit/delinquency story to enter public view by year-end 2026 or Q1 2027.
prediction🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediummortgage delinquency
So what: Media narrative shift could hit housing sentiment and related equities.
Mainstream outlets (Wall Street Journal and Realtor.com, which share a parent) have started telling boomers to consider selling and saying second-home ownership no longer makes sense, which Wright sees as a notable shift.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: lowWall Street JournalRealtor.com
So what: Sell-side housing media turning cautious is a sentiment tell.
Upper-K consumer and investor pain 6
Existing home sales show massive year-over-year declines in the $500,000 to $1 million segment ('middle of the upper K'), which had been propping up the median sales price.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highexisting home sales
So what: Loss of upper-middle buyers removes a key support for national medians.
Multifamily syndications where LPs (often doctors and dentists) aren't being paid are now pervasive as 2021-vintage borrowers hit a debt maturity wall without the refinancing they counted on; the same applies to commercial real estate and vehicles like Blue Owl.
causal🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highmultifamilyBlue Owlcommercial real estate
So what: High-net-worth wealth losses feed into reduced housing demand and forced sales.
“you're hitting that hard debt maturity wall” ▶ 38:02
Consumer spending is rising only because staples and gas cost more, with credit card balances growing because people can't afford everyday life; Home Depot and consumer staples companies are flashing warnings.
thesis🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumHome Depotconsumer staplescredit cards
So what: Nominal spending strength masks consumer stress.
Citing Parcl, Wright says for every two vacation homes sold only one is bought, as boomers offload non-essential properties in a risk-off move; luxury markets and spec homes (e.g. an unsold $25M+ spec flip) are struggling.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumParclvacation homesluxury housing
So what: Second-home and luxury markets face supply overhang.
“for every two vacation homes that are sold, one is bought” ▶ 39:09
Private credit is still lending on short-term rental investment properties, which is why more STR owners haven't sold yet; when that stops, selling will intensify.
causal🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumprivate creditshort-term rentals
So what: A private-credit pullback is a trigger for STR liquidations.
“because private credit is still flowing” ▶ 40:07
Wright expects a stock market correction based on what she saw on the road, which would push high-net-worth owners to list vacation homes immediately and worsen the housing story.
prediction🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumstock market
So what: Equity and housing downside are linked through wealth effects.
Practical housing playbook by life stage 7
For older owners with a 1-2 year horizon to downsize, Wright advises list, sell and rent rather than wait, asking 'who could buy my home?' rather than trusting automated estimates.
framework🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highhome value estimates
So what: Sellers with time pressure should get ahead of the decline.
“So list, sell, and rent would be the answer for most people.” ▶ 42:59
Rents are firming somewhat because people 65+ are the biggest cohort adding to rentals as they exit ownership, though concessions like three months free are still common.
data_point🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: mediumrents
So what: Rental market is not collapsing; downsizers support rent demand.
For first-time buyers, Wright says now is not the time to buy; use this as a planning period to pick location and home type and be ready when conditions turn.
framework🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highfirst-time buyers
So what: Patience favors buyers.
“No, this is not the time to jump in.” ▶ 44:40
For owners without plans to sell, Wright's priority is paying down debt as fast as possible, starting with high-interest credit cards, while keeping low-rate mortgages and watching for refinancing opportunities.
framework🗣 Melody Wrightconviction: highdebt
So what: Those with debt will suffer most in the coming downturn.
“those with debt I think are going to suffer the most” ▶ 46:28
Adam Taggart suggests young buyers use tools like Foreclosure.com and PropertyRadar to find distressed owners (liens, pre-foreclosure) and make below-market offers backed by Reventure data.
framework🗣 Adam Taggartconviction: mediumForeclosure.comPropertyRadarReventureNick Gurley
So what: Distress creates off-market buying opportunities for prepared buyers.
New Harbor's John Lod says housing is in the early throes of a broad-based selloff after years of distorting monetary policy, noting Massachusetts, considered resilient, now has more inventory, longer days on market and price cuts.
thesis🗣 John Lod (New Harbor Financial)conviction: mediumMassachusettsmonetary policy

Markets, precious metals and bonds (New Harbor segment) 8
Mike Preston says the S&P 500 has chopped between about 7,300 and 7,700 for six months with a textbook rally to the 50-day moving average and rollover, and the market is roughly three times normal valuations.
data_point🗣 Mike Preston (New Harbor Financial)conviction: mediumS&P 500Nasdaq 100
So what: Range-bound, overvalued market with failing momentum breakouts.
“we've been going between about 7,300 and 7,700 for 6 months” ▶ 1:16:30
New Harbor's system flipped back to full defense, cutting equity exposure from 50% to 42.5% to 38.5% and now 34.5%, adding call options and collars as hedges; healthcare and software were recent additions as relatively strong sectors.
data_point🗣 Mike Preston (New Harbor Financial)conviction: mediumNew Harbor Financial
So what: Systematic managers are de-risking despite blowoff-top possibility.
Preston still leans toward a possible blowoff top (citing Felix Zulauf's S&P ~8,000 call) and sees up to ~9,000 possible, noting the unusually rapid surge of fear on a 6-8% pullback may be 'loading the chamber' for a rally.
prediction🗣 Mike Preston (New Harbor Financial)conviction: exploratoryFelix ZulaufS&P 500

Silver (SLV) is down about 60-65% from its high and gold about 30%; Preston views it as a big shakeout within a roughly 10-year gold bull market only two years old, citing silver's ~60% GFC drop before a 5x move.
contrarian🗣 Mike Preston (New Harbor Financial)conviction: mediumsilvergoldSLV

Adam Taggart keeps dollar-cost averaging into precious metals, citing near-universal expert support for owning gold and the persistent loss of fiat purchasing power, and expects a 2027 drawdown rescue playbook to boost metals.
thesis🗣 Adam Taggartconviction: mediumgoldprecious metalsPorter Stansberry
So what: Metals as hedge against liquidity-driven rescue response.
Treasury yields of about 5.05% on the 10-year and 5.40% on the 30-year mean 'there is an alternative'; New Harbor expects potential capital gains as rates fall and sees the S&P priced for poor or negative 10-15 year returns, while Felix Zulauf sees the 10-year falling from 5% to 3%.

data_point🗣 John Lod (New Harbor Financial)conviction: mediumChristopher WallerDXYFederal Reserve

New Harbor monitors cash's relative strength ranking versus other asset classes; cash has ticked up as stocks, commodities and bonds faltered, a signal seen before major selloffs like 2008-09 and 2002.
framework🗣 John Lod (New Harbor Financial)conviction: lowcash
