Peter H. Diamandis 1 episode(s) · 50 insights

Anthropic's $72M Average Employee, the FAA's 4-Hour Ground Stop, and Starship Caught | EP #313119 min50 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Moonshots EP #313 panel (Peter Diamandis, Dave Blundin, Alex Wissner-Gross, Salim Ismail) debates Nvidia's post-Jensen governance crisis and eroding CUDA moat (with Google/TPU as beneficiary), Anthropic's ~$1.4T IPO and SF's unprecedented liquidity, a week of infrastructure outages framed as an AI cyber-asymmetry problem, and Starship's first tower catch driving launch costs toward ~$27-53/kg. Nvidia post-Jensen: governance, CUDA moat and Google's TPU opening 12
Nvidia's first quarter after Jensen Huang's resignation showed revenue of $115.8B (up ~103% YoY), data center revenue of ~$107B and net income of ~$65B, with interim CEO guidance of $130B for the next quarter, yet the stock fell ~1.5% the next morning.
data_point🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highNvidiaJensen HuangColette Kress
So what: Hypergrowth fundamentals are no longer moving the stock; governance/leadership risk is now priced in.
“Revenue was 115.8 billion, up 103% from last year” ▶ 3:12
A panelist argues Jensen's exit stems from a decade of the Nvidia board pushing for a succession plan he never executed (with family members inside the company), so the board concluded it could not run a $5T company that way and there is 'no coming back'.
causal🗣 Panelistconviction: mediumNvidiaJensen Huang
So what: Treat the Jensen split as structural rather than a reversible spat.
“we can't run a $5 trillion company this way and there's no coming back from that” ▶ 4:13
Alex Wissner-Gross expects activist investors (of the Bill Ackman genre) to circle Nvidia and try to overhaul a board he calls a governance embarrassment for America's most valuable company.
prediction🗣 Alex Wissner-Grossconviction: mediumNvidiaBill Ackman
So what: Watch for activist stakes / board fights at Nvidia as a catalyst.
“Watch for the activist investors.” ▶ 5:55
Polymarket odds put Jensen's return to Nvidia by end of next year at roughly 30%, and panelists argue every week of delay makes a return less relevant because it becomes very hard to come back empowered.
data_point🗣 Panelistsconviction: mediumPolymarketNvidiaJensen Huang
So what: Base case is Nvidia without Jensen; a comeback likely requires the company to deteriorate first.
“Every week that goes by, whether or not he comes back becomes less relevant” ▶ 7:07
Alex frames GPUs as the 2020s equivalent of 1970s oil and suggests great powers, including factions in the US government, may be playing games with Nvidia leadership over GPU export/import policy, akin to a 'decapitation strike'.
framework🗣 Alex Wissner-Grossconviction: exploratoryNvidiaGPUsUnited States
So what: Chip-company leadership is now a geopolitical asset; expect state interference and policy risk.
“Now the great commodity is GPUs 50 years later.” ▶ 8:34
Dave Blundin predicts that within the next year AI will 'obliterate' Nvidia's CUDA moat and that competing inference chips (e.g., Positron and others) look very strong; he'd advise Jensen to cash out his ~3.5% stake and return later Steve Jobs-style.
prediction🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: highNvidiaCUDAPositronJensen Huang
So what: Software lock-in is eroding; inference-chip challengers gain share as CUDA moat weakens.
“The CUDA moat is absolutely going to get obliterated by AI.” ▶ 9:42
Alex argues hyperscalers and frontier labs now have their own chip efforts (e.g., with Broadcom, in-house, or AI-designed architectures) and may no longer need Nvidia's design prowess, only TSMC supply, so Nvidia's alleged circular financing of neoclouds/startups may be what props up demand and could unwind under a cautious interim CEO.
contrarian🗣 Alex Wissner-Grossconviction: mediumNvidiaBroadcomTSMCneoclouds
So what: Key bear risk for NVDA: demand propped by vendor financing that new leadership may curtail.
“The AI has superseded however many PhDs Nvidia has on staff doing their chip design.” ▶ 10:36
Dave rejects the Ponzi framing of Nvidia's ecosystem financing, citing an unprecedented cash pile (~$130B next year at ~75-80% gross margin) and predicting Nvidia will end up paying a dividend, but agrees the board/CFO will fund the ecosystem far less aggressively without Jensen.
contrarian🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: Expect Nvidia capital return to rise and ecosystem investing to slow post-Jensen.
“They're going to end up doing a dividend which is insane.” ▶ 12:51
Dave and Peter argue Nvidia must act like Elon Musk and build adjacent companies up and down the stack (e.g., open source models) because its current GPU architecture won't be relevant 5-7 years out.
thesis🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: mediumNvidiaElon Musk
So what: Nvidia's long-term value depends on vertical expansion beyond GPUs.
Dave sees Google as a nascent vertical AI monopoly: more data than any government, plus exponentially growing TPU output, quietly positioned to fill the void left by Nvidia's turmoil at TSMC; Nvidia's weakening removes a counterbalance to Google.
thesis🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: mediumGoogleTPUTSMCNvidia
So what: Google/TPU is a major beneficiary of Nvidia's leadership crisis.
“that's a vertical monopoly like the world has never seen that is on the cusp of total AI takeover” ▶ 14:46
Peter Diamandis predicts that within 90-120 days Google (Sundar Pichai, Sergey Brin) will unveil a plan that shakes up the frontier-lab landscape.
prediction🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highGoogleSundarSergey

“next next 90 days, 120 days max” ▶ 16:00

contrarian🗣 Alex Wissner-Grossconviction: mediumGoogleGemini 4GCPxAICursor

Infrastructure fragility and the AI cyber asymmetry 6
Within six days (prompting Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy to push a $50B air traffic control revitalization): a Microsoft Outlook/Exchange outage (~130,000 reports across 74 countries), a 4+ hour FAA nationwide ground stop (longest in 25 years, ~12,462 flights cancelled), outages at Chase, Bank of America and Wells Fargo (blamed on a software bug/on-prem hardware), and a confirmed attack via a flaw in Optimizely that injected hostile JavaScript into 178 organizations including CNN, Zoom, HSBC, Equifax, Salesforce and IKEA.
data_point🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highMicrosoftFAADuffyChaseBank of AmericaWells FargoOptimizely
So what: Legacy centralized systems and third-party scripts are the weak points, not a coordinated mega-hack.
“injected hostile JavaScript into 178 organizations” ▶ 39:49
Alex argues AI has made exploits 'too cheap to meter', creating a new asymmetry against aging hardware/software that will force a 'great refactoring' of the whole infra stack; he cites Linus Torvalds' view that AI-discovered Linux kernel patches may have already peaked.
framework🗣 Alex Wissner-Grossconviction: mediumLinuxLenus Torvalds
So what: Multi-year demand for legacy-system rewriting and security modernization.

SpaceX/xAI: Starship catch, launch economics and data-center build speed 7
Starship Flight 15: Ship 42 reached orbit, deployed 26 Starlink V3 satellites, coasted 22.5 hours, and was caught by the Mechazilla tower arms for the first time; the Super Heavy booster diverted to an offshore landing.
data_point🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highStarshipSpaceXStarlinkMechazilla

Elon Musk outlined Starship V4: ~70 m long (~40% longer), ~60% more propellant, and nine engines with three more high-thrust Raptor vacuum engines.
data_point🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highStarshipElonRaptor
So what: Payload capacity expansion further lowers $/kg.
Peter's math: if fuel is ~25% of operating cost for mature reusable transport and propellant is $1-2M/launch, Starship costs $4-8M/flight, ~$27-53/kg vs the Shuttle's ~$54,000/kg (~1,000x cheaper), putting a 150 lb person in orbit for ~$2,000-4,000; Elon's revised $500K propellant and 200-ton payload imply under $1,000 per person.
framework🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highStarshipspace shuttleElon
So what: Orbit access approaching airline-ticket pricing opens new space markets.
“This is a,000fold reduction in cost.” ▶ 55:18
Alex frames reusable Starship as space's 'packet-switching moment': missions become hundreds or thousands of interchangeable, refuelable packetized flights (like shipping containers) rather than monolithic named missions, validating SpaceX's many-flight lunar architecture over Blue Origin's.

The AI Bubble Already Popped | TCAF 26983 min49 insights2026-11-20
▶ Watch on YouTube
Josh Brown, Michael Batnick, Jenny Harrington and Bin Talkington (as captioned) debate whether the AI bubble already popped (neoclouds, memory and Dell down ~50%, Nvidia shaken by Jensen Huang's exit) on the day of Anthropic's record ~$81B IPO, arguing the broader market is holding up through rotation into HALO/dividend names. Guests pitch Tesla (SpaceX merger thesis), Visa, GPIQ, Cisco, Freeport-McMoRan and Kimberly-Clark, and warn mega-IPOs typically draw down 30-50% in year one.
Did the AI bubble pop? Sentiment vs. activity vs. earnings · 15Anthropic IPO, LLM competition and AI routing · 7Mega-IPOs, private-market excess and retail access · 7Rotation, market structure and the 'Berkshire market' · 8Value discipline and the memory/old-tech round trip · 3Stock picks: Halo, income and copper · 9
Did the AI bubble pop? Sentiment vs. activity vs. earnings 15
Josh Brown argues the AI bubble already popped in July 2026 (about five months before the episode), centered on the neoclouds, which he considered bubbles all along and never owned; Dell, Micron and the RAM ETF have each been cut roughly in half while the Nasdaq sits about 15% off its highs.
thesis🗣 Josh Brownconviction: highDellMicronNasdaq
So what: AI hardware drawdowns of ~50% look like a burst bubble even though the index is only modestly off highs.
“And the AI bubble already popped in July. We're 5 months into” ▶ 0:33
Panelists distinguish a 'sentiment bubble' from valuation: memory names like Micron traded at single-digit P/Es, so the deflation was in sentiment, not in earnings-unsupported valuations, and stocks cannot rally amid such high skepticism.
framework🗣 panelistconviction: mediumMicronNvidia
So what: Low-multiple AI hardware can still fall when the sentiment premium unwinds; cheap is not a floor.
“There's no bubble there. It's just sentiment.” ▶ 1:19
A chart shown on the episode indicated leveraged technology ETF assets collapsed from roughly $130 billion to about $50 billion, cited as evidence the speculative 'activity bubble' has already burst.
data_point🗣 panelistconviction: mediumleverage ETFs
So what: Speculative leverage has been flushed, reducing forced-selling fuel from that cohort.
“I think it went from like 130 billion to 50 or whatever it was like a total and utter wipeout.” ▶ 1:35
Josh Brown argues money leaving AI is rotating into 'real stocks' people own that aren't AI (the HALO theme), e.g. Coca-Cola up about 40% in 2026, though he concedes defensive names can only go up so much.
thesis🗣 Josh Brownconviction: mediumCokeHalo
So what: The AI unwind is benefiting non-AI cash-flow names rather than dragging the whole market down.
“It's going into real stocks. Stocks that other that people own that aren't AI.” ▶ 1:53
Nvidia authorized an added buyback bringing total authorization to $350 billion, about 9% of its current market cap; Josh Brown says that makes a 'Nvidia to $1 trillion' scenario implausible since executing it would equal taking private a third of the company at that valuation, yet he still would not rush to buy the stock.
data_point🗣 Josh Brownconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: Buyback capacity limits downside, but leadership/legal uncertainty argues against bottom-fishing.
“I'm telling people I wouldn't rush into this stock and and buy this.” ▶ 4:25
A panelist calls Jensen Huang's departure from Nvidia a 'thesis violation', and adds that Anthropic's Dario Amodei reportedly said coding for AMD chips went from six months to three days, which panelists frame as an affront to Nvidia's CUDA moat.
causal🗣 panelistconviction: highNvidiaJensenAMDCUDADario
So what: Erosion of CUDA lock-in shifts bargaining power from Nvidia to its customers.
“So that's that's CUDA's that's that's that's an affront to CUDA.”
Josh Brown argues Nvidia cannot be 're-underwritten' because key questions are unanswerable: whether Jensen Huang or board members knew chips were reaching blacklisted companies in mainland China, how much China revenue survives, how much of the moat was Jensen himself, and how the market will react.
contrarian🗣 Josh Brownconviction: highNvidiaJensen WangChina
So what: When thesis inputs are unknowable, standard 'reunderwrite and average down' discipline breaks down.
“You can't answer did Jensen Wang or anyone else currently on the board or high ranking have knowledge” ▶ 6:38
Josh Brown says the market is signaling that buyers of compute (frontier model labs like Anthropic) have gained leverage over Nvidia, since they can now spin up AMD server instances in days, something they would not have said publicly while Jensen controlled global GPU supply.
thesis🗣 Josh Brownconviction: mediumNvidiaAMDAnthropic
So what: Pricing power in AI may migrate from chip suppliers to model companies.
“The market is telling me that it believes that the buyers of compute have gained maybe not the upper hand, but have gained hand.” ▶ 26:08
A co-host notes that in hindsight the market was sober about Nvidia, which traded at about 12x forward earnings versus roughly 20x for the market despite ~70% earnings growth, while memory stocks were rich despite ~3x forward P/Es; DRAM is down 52%, and Micron never got past 10x earnings, evidence of discernment and skepticism shaped by circular-financing worries.
data_point🗣 panelistconviction: mediumNvidiaMicronDRAM
So what: Cyclical peak earnings make low P/Es misleading; the market priced AI hardware as cyclical.
Panelists frame the key open question as whether there was an 'earnings bubble': AI company earnings (e.g., Google Cloud growth of 98% year-over-year) cannot keep growing at that pace, so the market derated those stocks on unsustainable future earnings.
framework🗣 panelistconviction: mediumGoogle CloudDellCore Weeave
So what: Derating reflects doubt about the durability of hypergrowth rather than current earnings.
“were we in an earnings bubble?” ▶ 13:23
Panelists argue a slowdown in hyperscaler capex could be bullish: if the Magnificent 7 (about 38% of the market) return to less cash bleed or positive free cash flow, the market would be majorly stabilized; Anthropic's IPO also shows the capex is 'money good' with public capital while OpenAI must still go to private capital.
contrarian🗣 panelistconviction: mediumMagnificent 7GoogleMetaAnthropicOpen AAI
So what: Less AI capex could re-rate mega-caps on free cash flow recovery.
“If Google and Meta Cash cash flow starts turning back up, the market's going to love it.” ▶ 15:08
Josh Brown says what saved the market after the Democrats' midterm clean sweep (with data centers a top exit-poll issue) was the realization that the hyperscalers already own the compute, making it more valuable if supply is constrained, and that the Street's refusal to reward capex hikes would push them back to positive cash flow sooner; Alphabet rallied.
causal🗣 Josh Brownconviction: mediumAlphabetdata centers
So what: Political limits on data-center buildouts can raise the value of existing compute holders.
“these are the companies that have the compute. So if we think there's going to be less of it than expected that compute is worth more.” ▶ 18:09
Bin Talkington (as captioned) cites seasonality that from November to the end of June after midterms the S&P has been up every time since 1942 (she says '21 out of 20 times'), averaging about 16%, because the election is a clearing event.
data_point🗣 Bin Talkington (as captioned)conviction: mediumS&Pmidterms
So what: Post-midterm seasonality is a tailwind into mid-2027.
Bin Talkington (as captioned) calls Fed Governor Waller's repeated calls for an immediate 50 basis point rate hike 'insane' and nonsensical.
contrarian🗣 Bin Talkington (as captioned)conviction: mediumWaller
So what: Hawkish Fed rhetoric is a live macro headwind she dismisses.
“For some reason, he needs to tell everyone we need a 50 basis point hike immediately. I think that's insane.” ▶ 17:33
Panelists suggest a cycle with a third-or-fourth-inning bubble deflation, followed by recalibration, is preferable to another 100% Nasdaq run, and that repeated shocks (Liberation Day, the war) may have let air out of the balloon and made the market more resilient.
framework🗣 panelistconviction: exploratoryNasdaqLiberation Day
So what: An early partial deflation could extend the AI cycle rather than end it.
Anthropic IPO, LLM competition and AI routing 7
Anthropic's IPO raised about $81 billion (largest US-listed IPO by deal size, ahead of SpaceX at ~$75 billion and SK Hynix at $26.5 billion, a redomicile), priced at $83 and traded around $97 on day one; Josh Brown bought it and argues a truly burst bubble would have left it a flop near $70.
data_point🗣 Josh Brownconviction: mediumAnthropicSpaceXSKHixAlibaba
So what: Strong demand for a mega AI IPO undercuts the 'everything AI is broken' narrative.
“would Anthropic who priced at 83 be trading at 97 right now?” ▶ 25:19
Anthropic's count of customers spending $100,000 or more grew from about 1,500 in December 2025 to 11,900 by September 2026, which a co-host says answers earlier doubts about monetization; the company only started making money in March 2023.
data_point🗣 panelistconviction: highAnthropic
So what: Enterprise AI demand is real and accelerating, supporting model-company revenue.
“it went to 11,900 from 1500 spending six figures to 11,900 in a year. The demand is unbelievable.” ▶ 27:46
Panelists expect multiple LLM winners (Anthropic, OpenAI, Gemini) for at least the next few years rather than winner-take-all like Google search, with enterprises flipping between or using all three, though the 5-10 year end state is opaque.
prediction🗣 Jenny Harringtonconviction: mediumAnthropicOpenAIGemini
So what: Avoid betting on a single model winner near term.
“I think it's going to be multiple players for at least the next few years” ▶ 28:23
Josh Brown says Google's installed base (around 11 apps with a billion users each) makes it a formidable gateway to push Gemini into Gmail, Docs, Sheets and YouTube regardless of user preference; Apple has similar distribution power but chooses not to use it.
thesis🗣 Josh Brownconviction: mediumGoogleGeminiAppleYouTube
So what: Distribution may matter more than model quality in consumer AI.
“They have, I think, 11 apps with a billion users each” ▶ 29:16
Jenny Harrington argues frontier labs are all building Ferraris while most LLM users need a CR-V, implying overinvestment in top-end models relative to typical use cases.
contrarian🗣 Jenny Harringtonconviction: mediumAnthropicOpen AIGemini
So what: Mass-market AI demand may favor cheaper, good-enough models, pressuring frontier economics.
“everyone's in this race to build a Ferrari. And the reality is is most of the people who are going to use LLMs, like they need a CRV” ▶ 29:52
Panelists say auto-routing tools (Microsoft's Copilot/co-work 'auto' mode choosing among Opus, OpenAI and others; Perplexity's neutral model picker) and agent harnesses will route simpler tasks to cheaper models, which is how enterprise AI spend will be used effectively.
framework🗣 panelistconviction: mediumMicrosoftPerplexityopus

“the harness is going to decide what to route where.” ▶ 31:22

Mega-IPOs, private-market excess and retail access 7
Bin Talkington (as captioned) warns that newly IPO'd companies historically suffer first-year drawdowns of 30-50%: SpaceX and SK Hynix fell ~50%, Alibaba ~50%, Visa 36% (2008), Rivian 90%, with Mastercard the rare exception; investors should use those drawdowns to buy.
framework🗣 Bin Talkington (as captioned)conviction: highSpaceXAlibabaVisaRivianMastercard
So what: Be patient with Anthropic and other mega-IPOs; better entry points likely come within a year.

Of the largest recent IPOs, nearly all are down from highs, including CoreWeave -62%, Lineage -48% and Cerebras -52%; SpaceX priced at $135, opened near $175, peaked around $225 and fell to roughly $105-107.

causal🗣 Bin Talkington (as captioned)conviction: mediumStripeSpaceXSorbane Oxley

contrarian🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: highRobin HoodKiyMichigan

“There is no way in real life that Khi is worth as much or more than Robin Hood.” ▶ 40:27
A panelist argues private-market firms resist IPOs because a public listing forces mark-to-market on comparable holdings, which could expose valuations far below book marks and crack the private ecosystem.
causal🗣 panelistconviction: mediumprivate markets

Rotation, market structure and the 'Berkshire market' 8
Michael Batnick notes Nvidia lost over $2 trillion in market cap in about 30 days while the market hung near all-time highs, arguing market-cap weighting self-corrects and that the claim the whole market was propped up by AI has been demonstrably false so far.
contrarian🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: Index resilience amid mega-cap tech collapse argues against calls to abandon cap-weighting.
“what's been proven to not be true is that the entire market was being propped up by AI demonstrabably false at this point.” ▶ 49:34
In March, when the Strait of Hormuz was closed, nearly every sector fell; in the current selloff real estate, utilities, staples and energy held up, and Michael Batnick calls such rotation the lifeblood of a bull market, while the spread between tech/semis average drawdown and the cap-weighted index has never been higher.
data_point🗣 Michael Batnickconviction: mediumstraight of hormutilitiesenergy
So what: Broad sector participation suggests an intact bull market despite AI weakness.
“And this is the lifeblood of a bull market as rotation.” ▶ 48:59
Bin Talkington (as captioned) says the equal-weight S&P (RSP) hit an all-time high just last week, echoing 2000 when excluding tech you did very well.

A dot-com chart shows tech and communication services fell 69% while the rest of the market was flat to slightly up peak-to-trough, the so-called 'Berkshire market' of housing, retail and non-internet companies, which Josh Brown likens to today's HALO trade.

So what: Daily factor swings create noise and entry opportunities in defensives.

No Priors · Elad Gil & Sarah Guo 1 episode(s) · 49 insights

Superintelligence Needs the Physical World with CEO and Co-Founder of Arda Bob McGrew60 min49 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Arda CEO Bob McGrew (ex-OpenAI chief research officer, ex-Palantir) argues superintelligence is bottlenecked by the physical world — compute, wet labs, factories — so automated AI research is a one-time S-curve, not recursive self-improvement. He details Arda's AI platform for manufacturing engineers (deployed across Anduril, aiming for seven-day automated line reconfiguration with arm-and-camera cells instead of humanoids), its Palantir-style FDE go-to-market, views on robotics foundation models, and a long-run vision of self-replicating machines.
Why RSI/hard takeoff fails: compute and physical bottlenecks · 8Intelligence is cheap; physical complements become scarce · 7Arda: making US manufacturing competitive via flexible automation · 11Arda go-to-market: Anduril, Cursor-for-manufacturing, FDE · 11Robotics foundation models, data and market structure · 7Long-term vision: physical singularity and self-replicating machines · 5
Why RSI/hard takeoff fails: compute and physical bottlenecks 8
Bob McGrew argues the 'automate the researcher -> runaway recursive self-improvement / hard takeoff' argument is straightforwardly wrong because researchers are not the bottleneck on AI discovery; experiments are.
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highRSIOpenAI
So what: Discount hard-takeoff timelines premised purely on automating AI research labor.
“this is, this is straightforwardly wrong because researchers are not the bottleneck” ▶ 3:54
McGrew says that in 2024, as OpenAI's chief research officer, the binding constraint was not researcher headcount but GPUs on hand — the physical amount of compute, whether it was accumulated in the right places and networked correctly.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highOpenAIGPUs
So what: Compute buildout and interconnect remain the real gating factor for frontier progress — bullish physical AI infrastructure.
“the actual limiting factor was not the number of researchers we could hire. It was the amount of GPUs that we had on hand” ▶ 4:17
McGrew's framework: model GPUs as running at roughly 35% of optimal efficiency at delivering discoveries; automating research could push that toward 90%, but going from 90% to 99% adds little — so automated research is a one-time S-curve, not a self-improvement loop.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highGPUs
So what: Expect a large but bounded productivity bump from AI research agents rather than exponential compounding.
“right now the GPUs are 35% of the way to optimal in order to be able to deliver discoveries” ▶ 5:31
McGrew suggests automated AI research could be as impactful as the jump from GPT-4o without reasoning to o3 with reasoning and tools — a huge one-time acceleration, but not RSI.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumGPT-4oo3
So what: Size the automated-research wave like the reasoning-model wave: big step change, then new bottleneck.
“That was a huge one-time acceleration, but it's not RSI.” ▶ 4:56
Elad Gil frames AI development as stacking successive S-curves of different types, a view that also applies to robotics and biology where the physical world limits progress.
framework🗣 Elad Gilconviction: mediumroboticsbiology
So what: Investment theses should identify which S-curve is next and where the next bottleneck sits.
McGrew argues even better in-silico simulation (e.g., 10x better experimental prediction in biology) yields only a one-time acceleration: the fast digital world 'eats up' the gain and progress becomes blocked on physical experiments again.
causal🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highwet labsprotein folding
So what: Simulation-first bio/materials companies still need physical throughput; wet lab capacity stays scarce.
“It's gonna be a one-time acceleration, but then we're gonna be blocked on the physical world again.” ▶ 11:59
Elad Gil counters that some physical domains (biology, chemistry, materials) may split off because strong in-silico models like protein folding can eliminate experiments such as solving crystal structures, unlike domains that require building physical capacity.
contrarian🗣 Elad Gilconviction: exploratoryprotein foldingbiology
So what: Distinguish simulable physical domains from build-limited ones when evaluating AI-for-science bets.
McGrew recounts that Ilya Sutskever would listen to an explanation of a cool new paper and simply ask 'So what are the results?' — illustrating that deep learning is fundamentally an experimental, not purely mathematical, science.
anecdote🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highIlyaOpenAI
So what: Even top intelligence cannot skip experiments; experiment throughput is the moat.
“Deep learning, which is, has no physical, you know, it, it's like a purely mental, it's a purely in some sense a mathematical science. Actually, it's an experimental science.” ▶ 12:41
Intelligence is cheap; physical complements become scarce 7
McGrew's economic framing: frontier labs produce cheap intelligence, so value shifts to its complements — wet labs, laid-out GPUs, perception and action; as hypotheses become cheap, the physical layer becomes scarce.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highwet labsGPUs
So what: Invest in the scarce physical complements to commoditizing intelligence.
“when hypotheses become cheap, then it's actually the physical part that becomes scarce” ▶ 7:35
McGrew doubts that Anthropic or OpenAI have a comparative advantage in building out wet labs, implying physical-world AI markets like bio and robotics need not aggregate into the frontier labs.
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumAnthropicOpenAIIsomorphic
So what: Room for independent startups in physical AI verticals rather than lab subsidiaries.
“do Anthropic, do OpenAI, do they have a comparative advantage in building out wet labs? Elad Gil: Mm-hmm. Bob McGrew: I think they probably don't.” ▶ 7:57
McGrew observes that despite huge gains from ChatGPT and agents, very little has changed in the atoms of daily life over the past 10 years; even Waymo is only 'halfway' to the physical world since riders are still bounded by traffic.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highChatGPTWaymoSan Francisco
So what: The next large value pool is AI that changes the physical world, not more digital productivity.
“probably the third time you're in your Waymo, you're like, "Traffic."” ▶ 2:17
McGrew invokes the O-ring production function: real-world outcomes like housing require every step, so speeding the digital parts barely matters when permits and construction remain the hard parts that agents cannot solve.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highO-ring production function
So what: Agent-driven productivity gains are capped by the slowest physical/regulatory step in a value chain.
“the hard part of building housing is getting the permits- Yeah … which no amount of agents are gonna help you do” ▶ 9:24
McGrew's bulldozer thought experiment: if data center buildout is blocked on bulldozers, the AI decides to build a bulldozer factory — whose first step is clearing ground, which needs bulldozers — rebutting claims that smart agents yield Dyson spheres within a few years.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highdata centersDyson spheres
So what: Physical bootstrapping loops impose real time constants on AI-driven buildouts.
“What's the first step in building a bulldozer factory? Well, you gotta clear the b- ground. What do you need? Well, you need more bulldozers.” ▶ 10:09
Sarah Guo notes Silicon Valley's 'abundance' concept is inherently physical — food, housing — and nobody experiences an abundance of digital work.
thesis🗣 Sarah Guoconviction: mediumabundance
So what: Abundance narratives only pay off with physical-world delivery.
McGrew paraphrases Dario's 'country of geniuses in a data center' and argues such geniuses are trapped if they can only exchange emails; today humans are the eyes and hands of data-center intelligence.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highDario
So what: Perception and actuation hardware is the bridge that unlocks superintelligence value.
“right now, humans are the eyes and hands of the intelligence that's in the data center” ▶ 52:22
Arda: making US manufacturing competitive via flexible automation 11
After a year interviewing manufacturing leaders, McGrew concluded America is great at designing products (iPhone, defense) but manufactures little here fundamentally because of labor costs; outside defense — where the government pays whatever works — cost-competitive US production requires automated lines.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArdaiPhoneAmerica
So what: Reshoring non-defense manufacturing hinges on cheap, flexible automation, not policy alone.
“if you're not in defense, then you need to build an automated line if you want to be able to build something cost competitively in the US” ▶ 14:16
McGrew says about 75% of an iPhone's parts change each year and only ~25% stay the same; that 25% runs on highly automated lines while the 75% is assembled by hand because today's bespoke automation doesn't generalize across generations.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highiPhone
So what: Product churn, not robot capability, is what keeps consumer electronics assembly manual and offshore.
“each year the iPhone 70, has 75% of the parts in it change. Only about 25% stay the same.” ▶ 14:31
McGrew contrasts cars — a couple of years and ~$200 million for an automated facility amortized over seven years of the same model — with iPhones, which would need to go from ~25% to ~90% automated to pencil, impossible to amortize unless automation becomes flexible.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highiPhone
So what: Flexible/reconfigurable automation is the unlock for automating high-mix products in the US.
“you have to go from about 25% automated to about 90% automated for it to pencil” ▶ 15:34
McGrew cites roughly a million people assembling iPhones or components in Asia supported by about 10,000 manufacturing engineers who design, stand up, and spin down lines — the role Arda aims to automate.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highiPhoneAsiaArda
So what: Manufacturing engineering is a concentrated, high-leverage labor pool for AI automation.
“There are about a million people assembling iPhones or components in Asia. There are, alongside that, about 10,000 manufacturing engineers” ▶ 15:56
Arda's goal: let a customer upload a product design into its cloud and design and reconfigure an automated line in seven days — matching what manufacturers consider very agile for even manual lines (a human worker can be retrained in a day).
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArda
So what: Benchmark for reconfigurable automation: seven-day design-to-line turnover.
“allows you to upload your product design into our cloud- And then design and ultimately reconfigure an automated line in seven days” ▶ 16:44
McGrew identifies defense, consumer electronics, and data centers/data-center supply chain (where chips change frequently) as the high-mix industries needing fast turnover, where almost everything is still built by hand.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highdefenseconsumer electronicsdata centers
So what: Target markets for flexible-manufacturing AI: defense, consumer electronics, data center hardware.
“those are the kinds of industries, defense, consumer electronics, the data centers, and data center supply chain” ▶ 18:28
McGrew notes that in Ukraine, battlefield changes now flow into physical design changes and back to the battlefield in only a few weeks, driven by competitive pressure with Russia.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highUkraineRussia
So what: Wartime iteration speed creates demand for rapid-reconfiguration defense manufacturing.
“you're now spending only a few weeks from changes in the battlefield” ▶ 17:31
McGrew says the form factor for replacing human line workers is two arms and a camera — often one arm and a camera — not humanoid robots, since Arda is replacing humans at stations, not automotive-style baroque robotics.
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highhumanoid robot
So what: Skeptical signal for humanoid-robot theses in factory assembly; simple arm cells suffice.
“two arms and a camera- Elad Gil: Mm-hmm … Bob McGrew: is enough. In a lot of cases, actually one arm and a camera is enough” ▶ 20:11
McGrew argues robot-arm dexterity is not the barrier: existing automated lines can build ~90% of these products; the barrier is economics because CapEx machines must be redesigned and rebuilt every year.
causal🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highCapEx
So what: The value is in the intelligence/design layer that cuts retooling cost, not in better arm hardware.
“It's the economics because those arms and, you know, that, those, they call them CapEx, capital expenditures, those machines have to be redesigned and rebuilt every year.” ▶ 21:08
McGrew distinguishes open-world robotics (cleaning a novel bedroom, long-horizon planning) from closed-world robotics (assembly lines, fully controlled, repetitive, requiring 99–99.9% reliability) and says Arda targets the closed world.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArda
So what: Closed-world factory robotics is tractable now; open-world home robotics remains much harder.
“In a closed world, actually, like 99 or 99.9% of the time, you gotta go construct the device” ▶ 25:37
McGrew likens traditional automation to a Rube Goldberg machine of chutes, vibrating cups and fixtures enabling perception-free deterministic picks; turnaround takes ~six months because half the work is machine design and half is calibration — AI design plus perception/RL-based agentic robotics can shrink both.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highRube Goldberg machineagentic robotics
So what: Arda-style agentic robotics attacks both design and calibration costs of line changeovers.
“half the work is in the design, the other half is in the calibration” ▶ 24:03
Arda go-to-market: Anduril, Cursor-for-manufacturing, FDE 11
Arda has spent its first year partnered with Anduril building a software and AI platform for manufacturing engineers, aimed at a single source of truth for the manufacturing process/plan and a reconfigurable factory that can spin lines up and down on demand.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArdaAnduril
So what: Anduril is the anchor design partner validating Arda's defense-manufacturing wedge.
Arda got access to Anduril's systems in January, had its first user in April, and that user completed a virtual build of an actuator in 90 minutes versus a few days normally; Arda is now deployed across all Anduril product divisions, from drones to missiles, and is working on the Fury collaborative combat aircraft.
data_point🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArdaAndurilFury
So what: Concrete traction: days-to-minutes engineering gains and full-division deployment at a major defense prime.
“he was able to do that in 90 minutes using our software” ▶ 27:10
McGrew positions Arda as 'Cursor for manufacturing engineers', but since no IDE exists for manufacturing engineering, Arda first built the data structure and a Git equivalent, then layered an agent on top.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highCursorGitArda
So what: Owning the system of record for manufacturing process is the moat before agents add value.
“There's no IDE for manufacturing engineers.” ▶ 28:22
Controlling the manufacturing process lets Arda move left (design-for-manufacturing feedback to design engineering) and right (designing automated stations for individual manufacturing steps).
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArda
So what: Expansion path from software system of record into DFM and automation design.
Arda is not training its own models: frontier models can already understand manufacturing processes and design lines, and act best as orchestrators of specialized tools such as motion planning and CAD inspection.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highArdaCAD
So what: Application-layer physical AI can win without frontier-scale model training spend.
“The frontier model is not adding all the value. They're an orchestrator of all of these different tools.” ▶ 32:42
McGrew expects a small number of very capital-intensive frontier models at the top, with specialist models justified mainly by cost; for non-frontier players, small special-purpose models that beat off-the-shelf frontier models are very approachable 'at least for now', akin to writing a tool.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: medium
So what: Specialist models are a cost/latency play; frontier capability concentrates in a few labs.
“I don't think you're gonna have specialist models unless you're thinking about cost” ▶ 33:15
McGrew contrasts Palantir's relentlessly problem-first approach with OpenAI's technology-first approach, recounting Ilya rejecting customer-led development after GPT-3 because 10x-ing training data teaches more than any one customer; Arda is deliberately problem-first.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highPalantirOpenAIGPT-3IlyaArda
So what: Frontier labs can be tech-first; application companies should be problem-first.
McGrew credits Shyam Sankar for Palantir's forward deployed engineering model, which Arda uses: embed engineers with customers and never build a feature without a customer ready to use it.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highShyam SankarPalantirArda
So what: FDE-led roadmaps de-risk enterprise product development.
“I would not develop a feature unless I had a customer that was ready to use that feature” ▶ 46:19
McGrew argues enterprise adoption hinges on a power user, not just the sponsor that Crossing the Chasm emphasizes; e.g., Sarah Adams was effectively Palantir's only user at an agency for about a year, and Arda's head of product sat beside its first Anduril user.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highCrossing the ChasmSarah AdamsPalantirAnduril
So what: Diligence enterprise startups for committed end-user champions, not just executive buyers.
“what the book doesn't talk about is you actually also need a user” ▶ 48:54
McGrew says big organizations are just assemblages of small teams ('OpenAI doesn't want anything. Sam wants something'), so enterprise expansion proceeds team by team, snowballing outcomes — e.g., from an actuator to missiles with 100x more parts to the Fury.
framework🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highOpenAIAndurilFury
So what: Land-and-expand via outcome-based wins is the realistic path into large manufacturers.
“big organizations are just an assemblage of very small teams” ▶ 49:21
McGrew says copycats misread FDE as deploying consultants; it was widely mocked 2015–2020 until Palantir became a $300 billion company, and it only gives leverage if FDE learnings are productized back into the platform.
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highPalantir
So what: Screen FDE-style startups for productization rate, otherwise they're services businesses.
“people started doing it when Palantir became a $300 billion company and started profiting” ▶ 50:52
Robotics foundation models, data and market structure 7
McGrew believes general robotics models like Astra and Fable work less because of more robot data and more because better models extract the last bits of signal from real-world data like internet videos, yielding representations that can be fine-tuned to robotics.
causal🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumAstraFable
So what: Teleop/data-collection moats may be weaker than assumed; representation quality from broad data matters more.
“the actual motor of progress here is not that they collected a lot more robot data than the next people” ▶ 40:33
McGrew calls teleoperation labs and UMI-style recording devices extremely scarce data sources, comparing it to training GPT-4 by paying Scale AI humans to write all the data — infeasible.
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumGPT-4scale AI
So what: Be skeptical of robotics businesses whose thesis rests on proprietary teleop data scale.
“if we had to pay, pay scale AI to go have humans write all of the data. Like, we would never have really been able to train GPT-4” ▶ 38:44
McGrew sees a tension: Astra/Fable-type models are strong out of the box but slow and hard to fine-tune, creating an opening for players like Pi and General that fine-tune on use-case-specific data — 'the bitter lesson' versus 'training on the test set'.
thesis🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumAstraFablePiGeneral
So what: Robotics market may not consolidate into labs; fine-tunable model providers have a niche.
“the bitter lesson is very strong, and also the idea of training on the test set is very strong” ▶ 42:14
McGrew says capable general robotics models 'really democratize' robotics, and in agentic robotics such models become one tool among many — e.g., a slow dexterous model only on the hardest station.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumFable
So what: Deployers that orchestrate multiple robotic tools capture value over single-model vendors.
“I think this really democratizes it.” ▶ 41:08
McGrew notes robots need fast clock cycles, typically achieved by distilling large models into smaller ones (e.g., Astra into a 'Luna' model), and that edge on-device models will be important, typically open source improved with data from big labs.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumAstraLuna
So what: Edge/distilled robotics models and open-source on-device stacks are a distinct opportunity.
McGrew says that after OpenAI's two-year project to make a Shadow humanoid robot hand solve a Rubik's Cube, he is 'permanently not a believer in robotic hands', and that a prior 1.5-year attempt to teach a robot checkers from vision convinced him robotics was too early in ~2015.
anecdote🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumOpenAIRubik's CubeWojciech Zaremba
So what: Operator skepticism of dexterous hands reinforces simple-gripper factory strategies.
“I'm now permanently not a believer in robotic hands” ▶ 37:27
McGrew pushes back on 'emergent capabilities' narratives, noting GPT-4's code-debugging iteration came from roughly two years of dedicated work by an engineer (Jerry).
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumGPT-4
So what: Treat claimed emergent model capabilities skeptically; they often reflect targeted investment.
“That's like not as emergent as you think it is.” ▶ 40:24
Long-term vision: physical singularity and self-replicating machines 5
McGrew predicts that over the next 10 years there will be a tremendous profusion of perception and manipulation forms — drones, sidewalk robots, flying, four-legged and burden-carrying robots — with every physical thing perception-enabled, cloud-connected and AI-controlled, rather than an army of humanoids.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highdroneshumanoid robots
So what: Broad bet on diverse robot form factors and sensorized devices over humanoid-only theses.
“Some people think that they will just be an army of humanoid robots. I think instead we're gonna see just this tremendous profusion of different forms.” ▶ 53:05
McGrew says factories that build factories already exist (machine tools); the change will be intelligently automating the enormous human work of designing factories and machine tools, which is required to support vast AI networks.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highmachine tools
So what: AI-designed manufacturing capacity is a near-term requirement for the compute buildout.
McGrew frames the digital singularity as itself physical — converting increasing fractions of the Earth's crust into data centers, possibly in space — directed by AI, and calls this a near-term, next-10-years outcome.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: highdata centers
So what: Data center buildout is the largest near-term physical manufacturing demand driver.
“part of the digital singularity involves converting increasing fractions of the Earth's crust- Mm-hmm … into data centers” ▶ 55:46
McGrew argues self-replicating machines (Von Neumann probes) are a better form factor than humans for spreading between the stars and will almost certainly happen; the priority is keeping humanity in control and benefiting.
prediction🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: mediumVon Neumann
So what: Long-horizon framing for space manufacturing and AI-control/alignment investments.
“they are a better form factor for spreading between the stars than humanity is” ▶ 56:20
McGrew proposes a Fermi paradox resolution: the most effective civilizations spread self-replicating probes near light speed, so we would only detect them shortly before arrival — or humanity may be the first such wave.
contrarian🗣 Bob McGrewconviction: exploratoryFermi paradoxDrake equation
So what: Speculative; low direct investment relevance.

AI demand vs. market skepticism 11
Gavin Baker argues the market's AI ROI math is flawed: it compares ~$1-2 trillion of capex against only ~$120B of Anthropic (~$80B) plus OpenAI (~$40B) revenue and under $200B total, ignoring open-source and enterprise on-prem AI demand.
contrarian🗣 Gavin Bakerconviction: highAnthropicOpenAIGemini
So what: AI-capex bears may be undercounting returns; open-source and enterprise AI demand is invisible in headline lab revenue.
“all we have to show for it is 80 billion in anthropic revenue. Um, plus the open AI revenue and you know, it seems like that's been reported to be around 40.” ▶ 58:46
Fireworks (serving and customizing open-source models) has seen its top customer grow 13x in the first nine to ten months of the year, per Gavin Baker relaying Fireworks executive Lynn Chow; Fireworks is an Atreides portfolio company.
data_point🗣 Gavin Bakerconviction: highFireworksatrades
So what: Open-source inference providers are a fast-growing, under-tracked demand pool.
“their top customer. is 13xed in the first uh nine or 10 months of this year” ▶ 59:31
Because margins on open-source tokens are lower, the open-source ecosystem drags proportionally more compute demand with it than closed-model revenue suggests.
causal🗣 Gavin Bakerconviction: mediumopen source
So what: Compute demand per dollar of AI revenue is higher in open-source, supporting semis demand.
“because the margins are lower on these open source tokens, it drags more compute demand with it” ▶ 1:00:02
Dell sold 3,200 'AI factories' to enterprises in 2024-25 and about 3,300 in the last nine months alone, meaning 6,000+ enterprises run open-source AI on Dell GPU servers, demand absent from the market's ROI calculation.
data_point🗣 Gavin Bakerconviction: highMichael DellDell
So what: Enterprise on-prem AI adoption is accelerating; bullish for Dell and GPU suppliers.
“in the first two years of offering AI factories, which is 24 and 25, they sold 3,200 of them. And they've sold, I believe, 3,300 in the last 9 months.” ▶ 1:00:22
Gavin Baker reports conference commentary showed AI demand strong and accelerating (Cerebras, SpaceX compute demand, a CEO whose business accelerated since earnings two weeks prior), while stocks trade as if there is an imminent demand problem.
thesis🗣 Gavin Bakerconviction: highCerebrasSpaceX
So what: Disconnect between ground-level fundamentals and AI stock prices could be an entry point.
“stocks are trading like there is an imminent issue with demand and I just don't think that's what we've heard here today” ▶ 58:00
Baker frames the market as in a Buffett 'voting machine' phase fixated on second derivatives (peak growth rate); if AI is like a 60-year railroad build-out, Nvidia and most AI stocks are cheap even at 5-10% annual growth beyond next year's guide, with 2027 guidance as the weighing catalyst.
framework🗣 Gavin Bakerconviction: mediumNvidiaWarren Buffett
So what: Watch 2027 guides; the 'growth peaked = it's over' heuristic may be wrong for long-cycle infrastructure.
“let's say they grow 8% a year 5% a year 10% a year it's a cheap stock it's a cheap stock”
Andrew Feldman says the market has swung toward valuing inference speed: a year and a half ago customers questioned why speed mattered, now daily users demand it, and Cerebras claims 10-15x faster AI compute than alternatives with demand 'off the chart'.
thesis🗣 Andrew Feldman (Cerebras CEO)conviction: highCerebras
So what: Fast inference is becoming a purchasing criterion, favoring specialized inference silicon.
“we build the fastest AI compute on Earth. Not by a little bit, but 10 or 15x.” ▶ 8:55

data_point🗣 Andrew Feldman (Cerebras CEO)conviction: highCerebrasOpenAIAWSMicrosoftOracle

“we we we have OpenAI as a customer but in the last 6 months we we won AWS and Microsoft and and Oracle pretty good six months.” ▶ 9:17
Feldman views the recent cyber/airline chaos as a once-in-30-years confluence of unlucky events rather than a new normal, and expects AI demand to keep 'ripping', models to improve, and open-source models to keep closing in with the open vs closed battle intensifying.
prediction🗣 Andrew Feldman (Cerebras CEO)conviction: mediumCerebras
So what: CEO-level read that recent selloff drivers are transient.
“I don't think people should think of of the tough last week as sort of the the new normal.” ▶ 22:23
Feldman argues a working system is weakest during an upgrade (e.g., the air traffic control failure during a copper-to-fiber or router change), and that data center failures like a recent Microsoft outage are rare once-in-a-decade events.
framework🗣 Andrew Feldman (Cerebras CEO)conviction: mediumFAAMicrosoft
So what: Migration periods are the risk window for infrastructure operators.
“a working system uh is at its weakest when you try and change it.” ▶ 3:59
Feldman argues data centers create high-paying multi-year construction jobs (hence labor-union support in many states) and, via cloud and AI, enable small-business and creator job creation.
causal🗣 Andrew Feldman (Cerebras CEO)conviction: mediumdata centers
So what: Counter-narrative to data-center local opposition; relevant to permitting politics.

AI-era cybersecurity 6
The Vercel CEO says the reward for hacking has risen because AI tokens are now near-currency fungible with dollars, with black markets for tokens and distillation attacks; targets are companies with token access, including North Korean actors.
causal🗣 Vercel CEO (G)conviction: highNorth Korea
So what: Holders of AI compute/tokens face elevated attack risk; security spend rises.
“Tokens are very precious. They're fungeable with US dollars, right?” ▶ 1:33:09
The Vercel CEO's hottest take: there will be many more high-profile hacks ('everything hackable will be hacked'), with possible CrowdStrike-like outages of critical systems in coming weeks and months, before AI defense leaves systems in a strong position.
prediction🗣 Vercel CEO (G)conviction: highCrowd StrikeDeepSec
So what: Near-term cyber incidents likely; demand for AI security tooling.
“everything hackable will be hacked” ▶ 1:39:38
He argues decades of human-built tech debt must be rebuilt in memory-safe languages and near-provably-unhackable ways, with adversarial agents (mixing proprietary and open models) auditing every commit; Vercel inserts such an agent in its deploy pipeline and open-sourced a harness called DeepSec.
framework🗣 Vercel CEO (G)conviction: highVerscellDeepSec
So what: Infrastructure rewrite cycle and AI code-audit tooling are investable themes.
“We can rebuild them in memory safe programming languages.” ▶ 1:29:54
A recent Chase-related breach came from a compromised third party embedded on the website front end, not Chase itself, per the Vercel CEO; open models were also used defensively (Hugging Face used them to analyze its attack).
data_point🗣 Vercel CEO (G)conviction: mediumChasehugging face
So what: Supply-chain/third-party scripts are the key web attack vector.
“it's not because Chase got hacked. It's the third party that got hacked” ▶ 1:29:08
Brian Armstrong says Coinbase scans every line of code with cyber-expert models before production, designs systems resilient to any single employee's laptop malware, and keeps 99% of crypto offline—underpinning custody for seven of the largest banks, most crypto ETFs, 140 government agencies and 50 of the top 100 hedge funds.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong (Coinbase CEO)conviction: highCoinbaseCoinbase Prime
So what: Flight to quality in cyber turmoil favors trusted custodians like Coinbase.
“we store 99% of all the crypto entirely offline, airgapped from the internet” ▶ 53:29
Armstrong recommends avoiding SMS-linked 2FA (SIM takeover risk) in favor of YubiKeys or authenticator apps, and says Coinbase Prime offers multi-signer withdrawals, liveness checks against AI deepfakes, and even in-person verification.
framework🗣 Brian Armstrong (Coinbase CEO)conviction: mediumCoinbase Prime
So what: AI deepfakes are changing high-net-worth security practices.
“Don't link it to your cell phone number because like sometimes your cell phone number can get taken over.” ▶ 54:09

data_point🗣 Keller (Zipline CEO)conviction: highZiplineWalmart
So what: Retail last-mile automation scaling; competitive pressure on human courier models.
“We're now expanding to 300 Walmart super centers in the first phase.” ▶ 25:43
Zipline has three-plus strategic partners each seeking over a million deliveries a day, turning its whole plan into a 'unit of sale'; it is growing 10-15% week over week and aiming to scale 3-5x faster, with ~25 charging sites in Dallas.
data_point🗣 Keller (Zipline CEO)conviction: highZiplineDallas
So what: Demand outstrips supply; the bottleneck is hardware supply-chain scaling.
“growing 10 to 15% week over week” ▶ 27:17
Zipline CEO Keller warns investors newly pivoting to hardware underestimate timelines—Tesla took 15 years and SpaceX 20 to reach meaningful scale—and that a project's performance equals its weakest supplier ('a zero anywhere in the chain' makes the whole project zero).
contrarian🗣 Keller (Zipline CEO)conviction: highTeslaSpaceXZipline
So what: Expect delays and real-world learning for hardware-heavy VC portfolios over the next 3-4 years.
“So if there's a zero anywhere in the chain, then the overall project is a zero.” ▶ 40:51
Keller notes even the best-funded companies, OpenAI and Anthropic, are finding data center builds hard due to regulation, permitting, community acceptance and supply-chain complexity.
causal🗣 Keller (Zipline CEO)conviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Data center timelines face non-capital bottlenecks.
Keller argues developing nations will choose between a US and Chinese AI/robotics tech stack, and the US should compete there to secure economic and technology leadership.
thesis🗣 Keller (Zipline CEO)conviction: mediumChinaRwanda
So what: Global South infrastructure is a geopolitical battleground for US tech exporters.

Why China Is Going After Anthropic107 min47 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Former NSC China director Julian Gewirtz explains why Beijing has singled out Anthropic as emblematic of US AI hegemony, argues China's AI policy is driven by regime security and US cyber advances (Mythos), and details compute constraints (limited Nvidia buying, Huawei push, remote access via Malaysia), an emerging Chinese open-weight risk discourse, and why US–China AI cooperation must proceed incrementally from parallel self-interest.
Beijing's campaign against Anthropic and US AI firms · 6Chinese AI labs, data leaks and the state · 9Compute, chips and leading indicators of Chinese AGI belief · 7CCP risk perception: regime security, Mythos and open weights · 8US–China diplomacy, deals and verification · 11Taiwan, Global South and information influence · 6
Beijing's campaign against Anthropic and US AI firms 6
Beijing has attacked Anthropic in official state media more repeatedly and directly than almost any other American company in US–China history, including a commentary headlined 'Anthropic has contracted the American disease' ahead of Xi Jinping's US visit.
data_point🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highAnthropicXi JinpingBeijing
So what: Anthropic carries elevated China political/regulatory risk and is a lightning rod in US–China tech rhetoric.
≈ “the headline was “Anthropic has contracted the American disease.”” paraphrase
Beijing treats all US AI labs as one monolithic project of American technological hegemony — calling Anthropic 'a monster bred by the American path' — so inter-lab differences in views matter little to China.
thesis🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highAnthropicBeijing
So what: Differentiated China messaging by any single US lab is unlikely to buy it distinct treatment in Beijing.
≈ “Beijing said Anthropic is literally “a monster bred by the American path”” paraphrase
Messaging by any single CEO, including Dario Amodei, is unlikely to change Beijing's view; the biggest levers on China's AI safety stance are its own domestic risk perception, followed by the US making a 'costly signal' by actually regulating AI.
framework🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highDario AmodeiBeijing
So what: Watch Chinese domestic risk discourse and US regulation, not lab comms, as drivers of any US–China AI safety thaw.
≈ “My own strong view is that the most important thing that could cause Beijing to shift its current approach to AI safety is their own domestic sense of the risks.” paraphrase
US rhetoric of total AI 'primacy' or permanent dominance is the least helpful language for winning Chinese cooperation, as distinct from simply wanting a lead.
framework🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumUnited StatesChina
So what: Policy rhetoric framing (lead vs. supremacy) shapes the feasibility of bilateral AI deals.
China's state media explicitly linked Anthropic's distillation report with Dario's call to 'pace the frontier' as one coordinated effort to make Chinese AI look bad and keep the US number one — a connection few Americans drew.
causal🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highAnthropicDario AmodeiChina
So what: China reads disparate US corporate actions as coordinated strategy; expect retaliation narratives to bundle them.
≈ “they connect dots in our system that we may not connect ourselves” paraphrase
Beijing's official line on distillation of American models is not denial but that it is normal practice which the US is twisting into something it is not.
data_point🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highBeijingUnited States
So what: Distillation-based IP disputes are unlikely to be resolved through Chinese concessions.
Chinese AI labs, data leaks and the state 9
Anthropic reported that user requests to Kimi (Moonshot) and DeepSeek models were being rerouted to Claude, including in one instance requests from a PLA official, exposing sensitive Chinese data.
data_point🗣 Tom Reedconviction: highAnthropicClaudeKimiDeepSeekMoonshotPLA
So what: Chinese labs' reliance on US models creates security and embarrassment risk that may trigger state crackdowns on such dependence.
≈ “requests from users to Kimi models or models from DeepSeek were being rerouted to Claude” paraphrase
The Claude rerouting episode reflects how breakneck Chinese catch-up efforts have produced sloppiness and dependence on American models that is deeply embarrassing to Beijing; the CCP conversations with Moonshot and DeepSeek CEOs were likely as political as technical.
causal🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumMoonshotDeepSeekCyberspace Administration
So what: Expect tighter state oversight of Chinese labs' use of foreign models.
In China, a Hugging Face-style AI security incident would be treated first as a cybersecurity and regime-security issue, with incentives at every layer against disclosure — the public might never learn of it, and a METR-style third-party report would be highly unlikely.
thesis🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highHugging FaceOpenAIMETR
So what: Information asymmetry: Chinese AI incidents will be underreported, so absence of news is not evidence of safety.
≈ “at every layer, the incentives in that system are not to disclose more information, and certainly not to disclose it publicly” paraphrase
A major AI incident in China could trigger a 'broader rectification campaign' bringing the sector under greater central control, likely without public disclosure.
prediction🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumBeijing
So what: Tail risk of sudden regulatory crackdown on Chinese AI firms, analogous to past tech rectifications.
Some American labs have a track record of disclosing incidents and occasionally constraining their own racing — a very low bar that Chinese labs have not cleared — giving the US labs an edge on safety credibility despite a US government that does not seem responsible.
contrarian🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumUnited StatesChina
So what: Lab-level asymmetry in transparency favors US labs in safety-sensitive procurement and policy debates.
≈ “very low bar, not a bar that the Chinese labs have cleared” paraphrase
Chinese lab staff interpreted the US lab employees' pacing letter cynically: 'The United States has been pacing the frontier for China for years' and they do not believe the US will slow down.
anecdote🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumUnited StatesChina
So what: US voluntary pacing proposals are unlikely to elicit reciprocal Chinese restraint.
≈ “The United States has been pacing the frontier for China for years.” paraphrase
Many Chinese AI labs are 'AGI-pilled' while the Chinese government has been much less so, though that is changing; the views shaping the CCP are increasingly internal reports rather than public essays, and differ from what DeepSeek CEO Liang Wenfeng might say on an investor call.
thesis🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumLiang WenfengDeepSeekChinese Communist Party
So what: Lab rhetoric is a poor proxy for state intentions; focus on internal policy signals.
≈ “many of the labs are. The Chinese government has been much less oriented that way, but I think even that is changing” paraphrase
Chinese AI lab employees complain constantly about government overreach; their key asks include compute, capital, and relief from new rules barring model-company employees and even family members from traveling abroad without permission.
data_point🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumChina
So what: Exit restrictions hamper talent recruitment (e.g., PRC nationals in the US weighing return offers), a drag on Chinese labs.
Chinese regulators blocked Meta's near-complete acquisition of Manus, a buzzy Chinese startup that had moved to Singapore — a founder's nightmare signaling that Beijing can kill cross-border exits.
data_point🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highMetaManusSingapore
So what: Chinese-origin AI startups carry exit/M&A risk even after redomiciling abroad; discounts for acquirers and VCs.
≈ “the Chinese regulators came and yanked the chain and shut it down” paraphrase
Compute, chips and leading indicators of Chinese AGI belief 7
Beijing remains significantly constrained on compute and sees itself as behind; Gewirtz thinks it unlikely China becomes much less compute-constrained.
thesis🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumBeijing
So what: Compute scarcity remains China's binding constraint; supports demand for chips via any available channel.
≈ “right now Beijing is still significantly constrained on compute” paraphrase
Despite the Trump administration approving sales of previously restricted Nvidia chips, Beijing is buying them in limited quantities because Xi wants domestic compute reliance (especially Huawei) and Chinese firms can access top compute remotely in third-country data centres such as Malaysia, plus smuggling.
data_point🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highNvidiaHuaweiXi JinpingMalaysiaTrump
So what: Approved China sales may underwhelm for Nvidia; Huawei benefits from policy-driven substitution; Malaysian data centres are a channel.
≈ “Beijing is buying them in quite limited quantities” paraphrase
Ding Xuexiang, a top Xi adviser coordinating compute and AI policy, reportedly told the sector that if forced to choose, go slower rather than depend on foreign compute — reportedly calling buyers of non-domestic chips traitors.
data_point🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: mediumDing XuexiangXi Jinping
So what: Political pressure caps Chinese demand for US chips regardless of US export policy.
≈ “if there’s a tradeoff between going a little bit slower and making sure that you’re not dependent on foreign compute, go a little bit slower” paraphrase
The best leading indicators that Beijing believes in imminent transformative AI/RSI would be material moves — centralising compute and aggressively buying American chips — rather than speeches from leaders like Wang Huning or He Lifeng.
framework🗣 Julian Gewirtzconviction: highWang HuningHe LifengNvidia
So what: Track Chinese compute centralisation and Nvidia import volumes as a signal of Beijing's AGI conviction.
