Macro Voices · Erik Townsend 1 episode(s) · 46 insights

MacroVoices #559 Ole Hansen: What’s Next After The spec Washout in Commodities56 min46 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Saxo Bank's Ole Hansen argues the 77% collapse in hedge-fund commodity net length (vs only ~10% index retracement) is setting up a 2027 rebound, seeing copper's AI-driven selloff as a speculative flush, refined-product/diesel tightness as the real inflation driver, and Brent's post-war normal at $70-90. He stays bullish commodities for 2027 with a metals tilt, while the trade desk proposes a copper bull call spread and flags Nvidia's muted post-earnings reaction, a strong dollar and Fed hike risk.
Speculative washout and positioning as a contrarian setup · 7Commodity super-cycle and 2027 outlook · 6AI capex fears versus electrification demand · 4Energy: Hormuz, backwardation and the refined-products squeeze · 12Metals: gold, silver, platinum and copper · 11Agriculture and El Nino · 2Macro drivers: inflation, Fed, dollar and equities · 4
Speculative washout and positioning as a contrarian setup 7
Ole Hansen says the combined hedge-fund net long across 25 major commodity futures collapsed 77% in roughly two months (COT data to Nov 10, 2026), back to early-year lows, while the Bloomberg Commodity Index retraced only about 10%.
data_point🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highCFTC COTBloomberg Commodity Indexhedge funds
So what: Positioning has been flushed far more than prices, leaving fuel for a rebound once the trend turns.
“we basically seen a 77% collapse in that length in the in the last two months alone” ▶ 7:07
Hansen views the collapsed speculative positioning as a reverse indicator setting up the next commodity run higher, but expects patience is needed: likely not before year-end 2026, rather into 2027, and only once a technical or fundamental catalyst appears.
prediction🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumcommoditieshedge funds
So what: Contrarian accumulation window into 2027 rather than an immediate trade.
“I don't think it's going to happen before a year end, but I think into next year then uh this is the setup the market needs for for a rebound.” ▶ 8:33
Hansen, a former hedge-fund employee, frames trend-following funds as buying strength and selling weakness via moving-average crossover signals, so current short/medium/long-term MA signals pointing lower are driving funds to cut longs and add shorts.
framework🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highhedge fundsmoving averages
So what: Spec flows are mechanical; price stabilization flips the signals and the flow.
“they buy into strength and sell into weakness and right now we are seeing weakness so they're selling into that weakness in order to follow the trend” ▶ 7:54
Hansen expects the catalyst for a commodity rebound to be inflation rolling over and the focus on rate hikes diminishing, especially if growth weakens enough that the Fed must weigh its dual mandate.
causal🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumFederal Reserveinflation
So what: Watch inflation prints as the trigger for spec re-entry into commodities.
In grains, speculative length in six soy and grain contracts surged from flat to about a million contracts during the July-August 2026 Black Sea/heat-wave scare and has now fallen back to almost flat, with corn flipping to net short from a record long and wheat net short as it has been for most of the last 4-4.5 years.
data_point🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highcornsoybeanswheatBlack SeaUkraine
So what: Ag positioning is washed out; downside selling pressure is largely exhausted.
“Just in in the six soy and grains contracts alone, it jumped from being flat to a million contracts. And now we're basically literally back to almost back to flat.” ▶ 34:22
Hansen says he is struggling to be bearish across the grain sector at current levels because liquidation is ending and catalysts like Russia-Ukraine or El Nino effects on the southern hemisphere could trigger a reversion.
thesis🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumgrainsEl NinoRussiaUkraine
So what: Asymmetric long setup in grains after the spec flush.
“So at these at these levels I'm struggling to be bearish in in across the grain sector.”
Hansen notes agricultural markets have thinner liquidity than energy and metals, which amplifies speculative surges and collapses when several fundamentals align at once.
framework🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumagricultural commodities
So what: Expect bigger overshoots both ways in ags than in energy/metals.
Commodity super-cycle and 2027 outlook 6
Hansen thinks the current commodity super-cycle, which he labels energy transition and electrification and dates from the 2020 pandemic low (prices up about 150% since), is potentially only at its beginning, following the 1970s and 2000s China/India super-cycles.
thesis🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumenergy transitionelectrificationChinaIndia
So what: Treat current drawdown as a correction within a multi-year bull, not the end.
“Well, I think we potentially only at the beginning of it.” ▶ 8:48
Hansen argues the cycle is underpinned by supply struggling to meet demand and by companies shifting from just-in-time to just-in-case inventory in a deglobalizing world, alongside structural themes like defense priorities.
causal🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumdeglobalizationjust-in-casedefense
So what: Structural inventory-building demand supports a higher floor for commodity prices.
Hansen says commodities have returned about 25% year-to-date in 2026, much of it from energy and from extreme backwardation benefiting passive long-only returns, while metals and industrials face headwinds from rate hikes, a strong dollar, China, AI data-center and tariff worries.
data_point🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highcommoditiesenergybackwardationdollar
So what: Roll yield, not just price, has driven 2026 commodity returns.
Hansen maintains a bullish 2027 commodity outlook but expects returns below 2026's ~25% because energy will not repeat; he expects a recovery in metals and thinks commodities may do as well as equities, which he doubts will match prior years' returns.
prediction🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumcommoditiesmetalsequities
So what: Rotate commodity focus from energy toward metals for 2027; keep commodities as a diversifier.
“So I'll be focusing on the uh on the metal sector” ▶ 15:01
Hansen notes sector leadership rotates sharply year to year (precious metals +80% in 2025 then -14% in 2026; energy -10% in 2025 then strong in 2026), so investors without a strong sector view should hold broad commodity exposure.
framework🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highprecious metalsenergybroad commodity index
So what: Broad baskets avoid the timing risk of picking last year's winner.
“If you want to be invested in commodities and you don't have a specific or strong view about an individual commodity or sector, then the broad approach makes sense.” ▶ 13:57
Hansen argues commodities are spot-driven markets set by daily supply and demand, so they are unlikely to be the asset that responds most aggressively to a macro shock such as an AI capex bust, even if short-term spec positioning anticipates it.
framework🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumcommoditiesAI capex
So what: Commodities should be less sensitive to an AI bust than equities beyond the short-term spec move.
AI capex fears versus electrification demand 4
Hansen says the AI capex bust fear is hitting commodities tied to power production such as copper and gas, but he sees the impact as mostly psychological and short-term because global electrification, EV adoption accelerated by high war-driven energy prices, and large ongoing AI projects continue.
thesis🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumAIcoppernatural gasEVsdata centers
So what: Fade AI-driven commodity selloffs where supply remains constrained.
“So I think the impact is more a psychological one here in the short term than an actual real one” ▶ 6:20
Hansen observes some level of fossil-fuel demand destruction in China, driven by EV adoption and high energy prices, whose extent will only become clear once markets normalize.
data_point🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: lowChinaEVsfossil fuels
So what: Structural oil demand in China may be weaker than pre-war assumptions.
Patrick reports Nvidia beat earnings after a nearly 40% stock collapse but the stock gave back overnight gains in regular hours, which he reads as the AI-trade downside distribution not yet being played out, with the Anthropic IPO reception as the next sentiment test.
thesis🗣 Patrick (MacroVoices co-host)conviction: mediumNvidiaAnthropicAI
So what: Selling into good news signals the AI unwind is incomplete.
“we're recording this before the anthropic IPO” ▶ 45:52
Erik Townsend frames the equity market's central question as whether the AI capex boom ends and crumbles due to a shortage of private credit.
thesis🗣 Erik Townsendconviction: exploratoryAI capexprivate credit
So what: Private credit availability is the transmission channel for an AI bust.
Energy: Hormuz, backwardation and the refined-products squeeze 12
Hansen describes the Middle East situation as no war, no peace: the market already prices a 2027 crude surplus, but he believes no solution is possible without Iran on board because as long as Iran cannot export crude it will keep disrupting the region.
thesis🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumIranStrait of Hormuzcrude oil
So what: Crude pricing may be underestimating unresolved supply risk.
“until Iran is being allowed to export it crude again, they will obviously continue to uh to create trouble” ▶ 19:22
Hansen's initial guess is that if Brent traded $60-70 before the war, $70-90 will be the new normal after normalization, still with some backwardation adding to returns.
prediction🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumBrentcrude oilbackwardation
So what: Higher structural oil floor; long-only still earns roll yield.
“if Brent was trading 60 $70 range before the war then 70 to 90 is probably going to be the the new normal” ▶ 21:50
Hansen stresses that inflation relief requires diesel (distillate) prices to fall, not crude, since consumers pay for refined fuels; distillates were still above $160 and gasoil one-year spreads showed prompt trading 25% above a year forward.
causal🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highdieselgasoildistillatesinflation
So what: Track diesel cracks, not crude, as the inflation signal.
“it doesn't really matter whether crude is trading 110 or 90 because that's what what feeds through to inflation is what we as consumers pay for fuel” ▶ 16:22
Hansen says 2026 energy-sector total return was 74% versus about 45% from flat price alone, meaning extreme backwardation added roughly 30% in roll income, even though natural gas was down about 25% year-to-date.
data_point🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highenergy sectorbackwardationnatural gas
So what: Curve structure can matter as much as price direction for long-only investors.
Hansen calls natural gas a dog to trade from the long side because its curve is in about 24% contango, while metals are in roughly 5% funding-cost contango and sugar, coffee and soybeans show small backwardation.
data_point🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highnatural gasmetalssugarcoffeesoybeanscontango
So what: Avoid long nat gas futures; roll costs erode returns.
“that's why natural gas is just a a dog to trade from a long perspective because of the the contango” ▶ 17:35
Hansen expects energy backwardation to persist into 2027 with a flatter curve but no shift to contango.
prediction🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumenergybackwardation
So what: Energy roll yield remains positive but smaller next year.
Hansen says refined-product and LNG shipments remain below pre-war levels because of missing refinery capacity in Russia and roughly 10% missing in the Middle East, so refined products, especially distillates, will be the last to normalize after any settlement.
causal🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: highRussiaMiddle EastLNGrefineriesdistillates
So what: Refined-product tightness and inflation pressure outlast any peace deal.
Erik Townsend argues a new permanently high crack-spread regime is underappreciated: destroyed refineries take a decade to rebuild and no one wants to invest, so crack spreads stay high; Hansen agrees but has not done deep analysis.
contrarian🗣 Erik Townsendconviction: highcrack spreadsrefineries
So what: Refiners/crack exposure may stay structurally profitable beyond the war.
“you blew up a bunch of refineries and it takes a decade to build another one and nobody wants to invest the money to do it. So I think the crack spreads stay high pretty much permanently.” ▶ 22:38
Hansen explains that refiners may cut runs despite high diesel cracks because gasoline cracks have collapsed to pre-war levels and a barrel yields only a fixed share of distillates, so much of the barrel is unprofitable; delayed maintenance from running flat out also constrains output.
causal🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumrefineriesgasoline crackdiesel crack
So what: High diesel cracks alone may not bring more diesel supply.
Hansen says Europe was left exposed because aging refineries closed without permission or appetite to build new ones, increasing reliance on Middle East supply that was then cut off.
causal🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: mediumEuroperefineriesMiddle East
So what: European diesel remains most vulnerable to Gulf disruption.
“In Europe, we uh we've been left a little bit naked once again.” ▶ 24:31
Hansen flags open questions for post-war oil: demand destruction, whether OPEC discipline holds or a market-share battle erupts now that the UAE has left OPEC, and how much China rebuilds stockpiles.
framework🗣 Ole Hansen (Saxo Bank)conviction: exploratoryOPECUAEChina
So what: UAE exit from OPEC raises risk of a price-war scenario after normalization.
Patrick sees crude, after a 50% rise off June lows to about $105 and a 20% correction in three weeks, as asymmetric: maybe $5 more downside but a return to October highs if flows through Hormuz are disrupted, absent an imminent peace deal.
thesis🗣 Patrick (MacroVoices co-host)conviction: mediumcrude oilStrait of Hormuz

Macro drivers: inflation, Fed, dollar and equities 4

Scaling Linear's GTM to $100M+ ARR — Cristina Cordova, COO84 min42 insights2026-11-19

Linear COO Cristina Cordova explains how Linear grew at least 10x in ~3.5 years with a lean (~170-person), no-SDR, in-house-support model, sequencing from startups to growth-stage to enterprise (launched 2025), and how it pivoted to agent orchestration after CEO Kari's 'task tracking is dead' post, with over 50% of issues now created by agents. Task tracking is dead: Linear's agent pivot 12
Linear's CEO Kari published a post declaring 'task tracking is dead'; Cordova frames it as Linear deliberately escaping the innovator's dilemma that pre-AI tools face, rather than defending a workflow (manual ticket tracking) that likely won't exist in a few years.
thesis🗣 Cristina Cordova (COO, Linear)conviction: highLinearKari
So what: Incumbent SaaS that cannibalize their own core category early may out-position larger peers locked into legacy revenue.

contrarian🗣 Cristina Cordova (COO, Linear)conviction: mediumLinearGoogle

data_point🗣 Cristina Cordova (COO, Linear)conviction: mediumLinear

Outage wave and AI-enabled low-skill hacking 9
A bad week for infrastructure saw two Microsoft outages, the first FAA outage since 2023 (blamed on lost frequencies, possibly new systems overloading old ones), bank outages and a rare Apple outage.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: mediumMicrosoftFAAApple
So what: Legacy infrastructure fragility is rising; resilience/security spend is a theme.
The Optimizely supply-chain hack injected malicious JavaScript into sites including Ledger, Zoom, HSBC and the BBC, acting like a card skimmer across a huge number of websites, yet reportedly netted only about $7,000.
data_point🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: mediumOptimizelyLedgerZoomHSBCBBC
So what: Third-party script supply chain is a massive attack surface; small losses reflect attacker incompetence, not safety.
“Ledger, Zoom, HSBC, and the BBC” ▶ 35:24
AI lowers the barrier to hacking, so many new attackers have no skills or plan, which explains fumbled outcomes like the Optimizely hack and the earlier Twitter takeover that only ran a Bitcoin doubling scam for about $100-160k.
causal🗣 hostconviction: mediumOptimizelyTwitterBitcoin
So what: Current low losses understate risk; skilled AI-native attackers will be far more damaging.
“most of the people who are now hacking things have no skills whatsoever and have no plan” ▶ 36:52
Taylor Monahan says DPRK's current AI use is boring, mostly optimizing existing workflows like social-engineering scripts and front-end/back-end/C2 code, but predicts a step change when AI-native attackers build campaigns AI-first from scratch.
prediction🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: highDPRK
So what: Expect a qualitative jump in attack sophistication from new AI-native groups; don't extrapolate today's mild AI use.
“we're not seeing the attackers optimize the AI yet. We will though 100%.” ▶ 43:52
People who were oversold dramatic AI-hacking narratives and then saw mundane reality are wrongly writing off AI cyber capability; the hosts argue you must constantly update priors (analogy: Tesla Roadster delays don't mean it never ships).
contrarian🗣 Taylor Monahan / hostconviction: mediumDPRKTesla
So what: Skeptics underrating AI offense creates a mispriced risk in security posture.
“You just have to constantly adjust your priors” ▶ 42:24
Austin Griffith argues AI is the savior, not the attacker: the problem is human-written code being hacked by increasingly capable AI, and society is in a transition until people trust AI to write safer code than humans (e.g. autonomous trucks, surgery software).
thesis🗣 Austin Griffithconviction: mediumFAA
So what: Long-run demand shifts to AI-written and AI-verified software; normie trust is the lagging constraint.
“AI is the savior here in a lot of ways.” ▶ 0:00
Austin Griffith estimates around 95% of normies would still prefer flight-tracking software written by humans rather than AI, despite evidence AI agents are outperforming humans at attacking software.

Crypto security: audits, monitoring and circuit breakers 10
Austin Griffith's '$1 audit' meme is meant to show AI auditing should be a CI/CD pipeline step, yet he still advises teams to get a $20,000 human audit because models aren't yet better than audit firms using Mythos plus their own tools.
thesis🗣 Austin Griffithconviction: highMythos
So what: AI audits complement rather than replace paid audits for now; audit firms that adopt AI keep an edge.
“you should be using AI in your pipeline to audit your contract and it should be more of like a CI/CD thing rather than a $20,000 audit” ▶ 47:48
Frontier-level AI was needed to find the edge cases in the heavily reviewed Balancer contract, showing human eyeball brute-force auditing is fallible.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: mediumBalancer
So what: Even widely reviewed DeFi contracts have latent bugs only frontier AI surfaces.
Audit capacity in crypto is cyclical: few engineers have the right mindset, audit firms boom (charging up to ~$100k for an ERC20 audit in the 2017 ICO era) then disband in busts, so few audit teams persist.
framework🗣 hostconviction: mediumERC20
So what: Audit quality and availability is pro-cyclical, worsening risk at cycle peaks.

data_point🗣 Austin Griffithconviction: exploratoryEthereum

Over the past six months Taylor Monahan's number-one advice to crypto teams shifted to monitoring and detection, cranking visibility through the roof because many low-skill agents poke at systems and can stumble into access.
thesis🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: high
So what: Monitoring/detection tooling is now the highest-ROI security spend for crypto teams.
“right now my advice is like monitoring” ▶ 52:35
AI lets small teams effectively monitor all systems and flag anomalies, a capability that previously required highly skilled security staff; teams should also red team their Web2 stack aggressively.

“The AI is so good at being like that's anomalous.” ▶ 54:41
Taylor Monahan's framework: assume audits cover ~90% of risk and add an autonomous circuit-breaker layer (e.g. withdrawal-only mode if more than $200 million tries to leave) to cover the long tail no human or AI would find.
framework🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: high

“Under no circumstances should $200 million ever leave this contract” ▶ 59:25
A contrarian take: audits catch a lot but never catch the thing that kills you, and formal verification only adds a few percent, leaving residual catastrophic risk.
contrarian🗣 hostconviction: medium

“But they never catch the thing that kills you.” ▶ 1:00:39
A protocol reportedly used a 'guardian mode' circuit breaker to freeze withdrawals and recovered about $5 million, cited as live evidence circuit breakers work.

So what: Circuit breakers have demonstrated real loss recovery.
Crypto has shifted from preferring immutable contracts with no escape hatch toward accepting kill switches with multiple gates; circuit breakers can themselves be immutable code, contrasting with the DAO hack where teams raced to fix the chain, not the bug.

Decouple · Chris Keefer 1 episode(s) · 39 insights

The Limits of Nuclear Technology Transfer w/ Michael Seely75 min39 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Nuclear engineer Michael Seely (Atomic Blender) and Chris Keefer trace how reactor technology transfer works and why it is so sticky: France and Germany fully indigenized Westinghouse PWR tech, Korea still depends on Westinghouse-owned Combustion Engineering IP (constraining exports like Barakah), and China escaped AP1000 export limits by stretching it past a 1,350 MW contract threshold into the CAP1400. Core lesson: vendor lock-in via safety-analysis codes and data lasts 60-100 years, so buyers must set tech-transfer goals at contract time.
Who can actually design and export reactors · 5Westinghouse IP leverage over Korea · 13France and Germany: paths to independence · 6Vendor lock-in economics · 9China's AP1000 end-run and other lineages · 6
Who can actually design and export reactors 5
Nuclear reactor design-and-export capability is as concentrated as commercial jet engines: currently deployed power reactor lineages originate essentially from the US, Russia and Canada, with France, Germany, China and partially Korea/Japan as secondary indigenizers.
framework🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: highUnited StatesRussiaCanadaFranceGermanyChinaKorea
So what: The pool of credible reactor exporters is tiny, giving incumbent IP holders durable pricing and gatekeeping power.
“in the case of of you know nuclear power reactors I think we can make the case um in terms of what's currently deployed that that's you know basically the US and and Russia and Canada” ▶ 2:26
Behind each simple nuclear component (a fuel pin, a pump, a trip signal) sits decades of encased R&D, materials testing and engineering know-how from vendors and labs, which is what makes technology transfer so slow and sticky.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highWestinghouse
So what: Reactor vendor moats are knowledge/data moats, not just manufacturing capacity.
“eventually comes down into something relatively simple like a fuel pin or a pump or the way a trip signal works in a plant” ▶ 5:35
Only GE and Rolls-Royce (plus France via a Safran joint venture) have full-spectrum commercial turbofan capability; the hosts use this as the analogy for nuclear, where many countries operate and maintain but very few can design and independently export.
framework🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: mediumGERolls-RoyceSafran
So what: Operators vs. full-spectrum OEMs is the key distinction for evaluating any nuclear nation or vendor.
Britain once had its own reactor lineage (Magnox and gas-cooled reactors) but it 'really didn't go anywhere', leaving it out of the current exporter set.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumBritainMagnus
So what: Indigenous designs can die without a scale fleet and export success.
Sweden's ASEA developed BWRs independently of General Electric and, per Keefer, Sweden also localized enough PWR design to export to Finland.
data_point🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: lowSwedenASEAGeneral ElectricFinland
So what: Smaller nations can break vendor IP holds with sustained engineering investment.
Westinghouse IP leverage over Korea 13
Korea received full tech transfer of Combustion Engineering's design in the late 1980s (based on Arkansas Nuclear One Unit 2, a ~1000 MW unit, plus Palo Verde steam-supply elements), creating the Korean Standard Nuclear Plant later rebranded OPR1000.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highKoreaCombustion EngineeringArkansas Nuclear OnePalo VerdeKSNPOPR 1000
So what: Korea's whole fleet descends from CE IP now owned by Westinghouse.
“they take the uh uh the Westing or sorry the combustion engineering basically what is the uh A&O2 uh design Arkansas nuclear 1 unit 2. It's a 1000 megawatt reactor.” ▶ 39:53
Combustion Engineering was financially weak when it sold the tech to Korea: bespoke non-repeated designs had produced design problems and heavy warranty claims, which let the Koreans negotiate a good full-transfer deal.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highCombustion EngineeringKorea
So what: Distressed vendors give away IP cheaply; buyers should time negotiations to vendor weakness.
“They have had some what you might call design problems that have resulted in a lot of warranty claims on their reactors.” ▶ 39:18
The APR1400 at Barakah is not enormously different from Palo Verde in Arizona: same power level, roughly the same fuel design and number of assemblies, and similar steam generator construction and NSSS layout.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highAPR1400BarakaPalo VerdeUAE
So what: Westinghouse's IP claim on Korean exports is substantive, not merely legal posturing.
“it's not enormously different from, for example, the Palaverde plant in Arizona. It's the same power level, same more or less the same fuel design, same number of assemblies.” ▶ 12:39
Because Westinghouse bought Combustion Engineering, Korea had to negotiate with Westinghouse for the Barakah export; Westinghouse could otherwise block it via Korean or US courts, and ended up supplying components and services and holding significant influence over the design.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highWestinghouseKEPCOBarakaUAECombustion Engineering
So what: Westinghouse effectively collects a toll on Korean reactor exports.
“you either need to pay us for that or we can effectively block you from uh participating in this.” ▶ 44:13
By the UAE disqualifying Westinghouse early in its bidding, Westinghouse still ended up with low-risk, high-revenue work on Barakah while the Koreans bore the construction risk.
contrarian🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: mediumWestinghouseUAEBarakaKorea
So what: IP-holder royalties/services on a partner's build can be a better business than being prime contractor.
“Westinghouse in the end ends up getting this beautiful lowrisk high revenue work um with the with the project that the Koreans are sort of taking on the construction risk for.”
Nearly 40 years after the transfer, Korean work on CE-derived plants is still often co-authored or supported by Westinghouse, unlike France which became independent within a decade or two.
thesis🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highKoreaWestinghouseFrance
So what: Korean vendor independence from Westinghouse is weaker than headlines suggest; expect continued Westinghouse participation in Korean deals.
“even almost 40 years later at this point after the technology transfer was originally initiated.” ▶ 0:08
Seely attributes Korea's continued reliance partly to risk aversion: staying in the Westinghouse 'box' lets operators blame Westinghouse if something goes wrong, whereas self-made changes leave no one else to blame.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumKoreaWestinghouse
So what: Cultural/institutional incentives, not capability, slow Korean indigenization.
“I just don't know if they're maybe a little bit unsure of themselves or unwilling to try and get out of this Westinghouse box” ▶ 51:51
Korea built KAERI and became very good at building and operating plants but was reluctant to design its own version, partly because export ambitions were weak in the 1980s-90s; only as CE agreements expired in the late 2000s did Korea pursue exports.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumKoreaKAERICombustion Engineering
So what: Indigenization requires deliberate design ambition, not just a build fleet.
Westinghouse and KEPCO signed a global agreement dividing markets, excluding the Koreans from Europe (except the Czech deal already signed) and the US, which Keefer calls a 'Westinghouse Monroe Doctrine' that was unpopular in Korea.
data_point🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: highWestinghouseKEPCOKoreaEurope
So what: Korean vendors are structurally capped from the richest Western markets absent renegotiation.
“we've got the Westinghouse Monroe Doctrine.” ▶ 35:38
A more recent US-Korea nuclear deal reportedly involves eight Korean-funded reactors in the US including two APR1400s, which Keefer frames as a major reversal of the Westinghouse-KEPCO market-division agreement, though its post-midterm fate is uncertain.
prediction🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: exploratoryKoreaUnited StatesAPR1400Westinghouse
So what: Potential US market entry for Korean designs; watch implementation risk.
There are now about as many or more Korean CE-design plants than Combustion Engineering itself ever built, with US CE units like San Onofre and the Yankee plants shut down, making Korea the front-runner for CE technology.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highKoreaCombustion EngineeringSan Onofre
So what: The center of gravity of the CE lineage has shifted to Korea even though IP sits with Westinghouse.
“it's you know kind of happy to see that your uh your children have gone off and done far better than you ever did.” ▶ 47:48
Combustion Engineering passed to ABB for about a decade with little investment and was bought cheaply by BNFL-owned Westinghouse around 2001, largely as a fuel and services business; Keefer muses Korea could have bought it, and Seely sees no obvious US blocking reason since Westinghouse itself was foreign-owned.
anecdote🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumCombustion EngineeringABBWestinghouseBNFLKorea
So what: A cheap missed acquisition now costs Korea export freedom; IP ownership of distressed assets can become hugely valuable.
Even had Korea bought Combustion Engineering, the tacit knowledge resided in US-based engineers in Connecticut who would not simply relocate to Korea, so ownership would not equal capability.
thesis🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: mediumCombustion EngineeringKorea
So what: Tacit know-how, not legal title, is the real asset in nuclear M&A.
France and Germany: paths to independence 6
France indigenized the Westinghouse PWR in phases: initial Westinghouse builds, then repeated 900 MW-class (three-loop) series builds, then N4 with its own digital I&C, culminating in the fully indigenous EPR exportable without tribute to Westinghouse.
framework🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highFranceWestinghouseN4EPRFramatomeEDFCEA
So what: Fleet-mode repetition plus national labs is the proven recipe for vendor independence.
“they do end up with their own design that they can then export quite freely without any kind of tribute back to Westinghouse.” ▶ 29:30
France paid royalties and licensing payments to Westinghouse up until the 1990s, while Westinghouse marketing still claims essentially the whole French fleet as Westinghouse-derived.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highFranceWestinghouse
So what: Licensing deals can produce decades of royalty income for the originating vendor.
“the French are paying royalties and uh uh you know payments to Westinghouse, licensing payments to Westinghouse up until the 1990s.”
Germany's Siemens/Kraftwerk Union decided early to go independent of Westinghouse, developing a clean-sheet PWR with different materials optimized for German regulation and maintenance, and exported it to Argentina, Brazil and Iran.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highGermanySiemensKraftwerk UnionWestinghouseArgentinaBrazilIran
So what: Clean-sheet redesign is the route to unencumbered export rights.
“they end up with a design that is truly uniquely German” ▶ 11:23
To claim independent IP, engineers must build from public-domain knowledge (conference papers, general concepts) and be able to demonstrate in court that a similar feature was independently developed; jurisdiction matters, and most US companies have given up protecting IP in China.
framework🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumWestinghouseChinaGermanyUnited States
So what: IP disputes over derivative reactors hinge on provenance evidence and venue.
“I think in China, most US companies now have kind of given up on that approach of trying to protect their IP in China.” ▶ 18:29
A 1970s German PWR sale to the Soviet Union for Kaliningrad failed not on Westinghouse IP but because the US (and likely UK) vetoed it via COCOM's unanimous export-control mechanism, partly over dual-use concerns like welding techniques useful for naval propulsion.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highGermanySoviet UnionCOCOMUnited States
So what: Geopolitical export controls can kill commercially sound reactor deals regardless of IP ownership.
“it's a political decision not to export an indigenous German design” ▶ 21:58
Building a national research lab (as France did with CEA and Korea with KAERI) is a prerequisite for ever getting anything other than an off-the-shelf design from a vendor.
framework🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highFranceCEAKAERI
So what: Watch whether newcomer nuclear nations fund labs; it signals long-term vendor independence.
Vendor lock-in economics 9
Vendor lock-in arises because any significant plant change (new pump, I&C replacement) requires re-demonstrating safety analysis to the regulator, which needs the vendor's validated computer codes, methodology and materials data.
causal🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highWestinghouseGeneral Electric
So what: Reactor vendors earn recurring, captive engineering-services revenue for decades after sale.
“the vendor has the people, the methodology, the computer codes in order to do that analysis” ▶ 32:54
Small fleets like Argentina's two plants rationally rely on vendors for once-every-20-to-30-years tasks like I&C replacement because maintaining that in-house capability is impractical, but this forecloses designing their own reactors.
framework🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highArgentina
So what: Small-fleet buyers are structurally locked in; large-fleet buyers can break free.
“That something that you do maybe once every 20 or 30 years. It it's not practical for them to do that.” ▶ 33:32
Vendor safety-case data includes things like tens of thousands of hours of corrosion testing (e.g. 50,000 hours) across condition envelopes, which regulators in every importing country require.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: medium
So what: The data moat is expensive to replicate, protecting incumbents.
“we've done, you know, 50,000 hours of testing under, you know, this envelope of conditions” ▶ 31:41
Russia's Rosatom uses exports to create roughly 100-year dependence spanning operations, analysis, spent fuel and decommissioning; Turkey's build-own-operate Rosatom plant represents the zero-tech-transfer extreme.
thesis🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: highRosatomRussiaTurkey
So what: Reactor exports are geopolitical alignment tools; buyer choice is primarily East vs. West.
Seely says the primary driver of reactor technology selection is East vs. West alignment, and that Westinghouse has only recently, under a more aggressive US administration, begun leveraging itself politically in the way Russia does.
thesis🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumWestinghouseRussiaUnited States
So what: US government backing is becoming a sales lever for Westinghouse exports.
“the primary driver for different countries making a technology selection uh is either east and west” ▶ 1:04:04
Reactor buyers should decide at contract time whether they want just power, operational self-sufficiency, modification ability, or eventual export capability, since plants last 60-100 years and this shapes contracts and institutions.
framework🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highWestinghouseGERosatom
So what: Tech-transfer terms are a key value driver to scrutinize in any new reactor deal.
“a lot of these things are going to be around for 60, 80, 100 years.” ▶ 1:10:50
Startups heavily dependent on their IP may be unwilling to do the kind of full technology transfer Westinghouse gave China, and their ability to provide decades of OEM support is an open question.
prediction🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumWestinghouseChina
So what: Assess SMR startups on long-term support capacity and IP-transfer posture, not just first-of-a-kind build.
“especially startups where they're very heavily dependent on their IP, are they going to be willing to do that?” ▶ 1:13:42
Keefer contrasts NuScale's export ambitions (e.g. Romania) with juggernauts like Kraftwerk Union that built many domestic units before exporting, questioning whether startups have the pedigree to credibly perform decades of OEM analysis duties.
contrarian🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: mediumNew ScaleRomaniaKraftwerk Union
So what: Skepticism on startup vendors' credibility as long-term OEMs.
Keefer suggests the nuclear bubble may be waning as the AI bubble shows signs of instability, and Seely's consulting work targets cutting through hype on which reactor technologies will actually advance.
prediction🗣 Chris Keefer (host)conviction: exploratoryAI
So what: Nuclear equity hype may be tied to AI capex sentiment.
“maybe the the nuclear uh bubble is is somewhat waning as the uh AI bubble is also showing signs of being a little bit unstable.” ▶ 1:12:53
China's AP1000 end-run and other lineages 6
China took a 'buffet' approach, buying VVERs, CANDUs, Westinghouse PWRs, EPRs and French three-loop designs, learning broadly, then producing Hualong One, which is sufficiently indigenous that Westinghouse and Framatome have made no IP claims on its exports (e.g. to Pakistan).
thesis🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highChinaHualong OneWestinghouseFramatomePakistanEPR
So what: China now has an unencumbered export reactor competing with Western vendors.
“Westinghouse and Frammatome have not laid any kind of intellectual property claims on the how long one exports.” ▶ 14:21
Westinghouse's 2007-08 AP1000 sale of four units to China gave full tech transfer for unlimited domestic builds but no export, with any derivative above 1,350 MW deemed no longer an AP1000.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highWestinghouseAP1000ChinaToshiba
So what: Contract thresholds can be engineered around; the 1,350 MW clause handed China its own design.
“there is a power designation on it of 1,350 megawws. Anything above that, even if it's derived from the AP-1000, is considered sufficiently different” ▶ 58:41
Westinghouse insiders believed the already-stretched AP1000 (upscaled from AP600) could not be stretched to 1,350-1,400 MW, but China did it with the CAP1400, building two in about five years and freeing itself to call it a domestic design.
contrarian🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highCAP1400AP1000AP600ChinaWestinghouse
So what: Underestimating a licensee's engineering capability destroyed Westinghouse's expected China upside.
“It the design really can't support it. It's already a bit stretched as it is. Obviously, that's not how that has played out.” ▶ 59:36
Westinghouse expected the China AP1000 deal to be its 'new France' with a piece of ~50 reactors, but China built four, learned, then built domestic versions without Westinghouse in construction or component supply, making the deal look poor in retrospect versus the decades of French royalties.
thesis🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: highWestinghouseChinaAP1000ToshibaFrance
So what: Full tech transfer to a large capable buyer can cannibalize the vendor's future revenue.
“doesn't look like such a good deal for Westinghouse anymore.” ▶ 1:00:59
Soviet VVER exports never spawned independent derivatives; the closest was Czechoslovakia's Škoda, which manufactured VVER-440s to QA requirements as a 'clone factory' without design work.
data_point🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumVVERCzechoslovakiaSoviet Union
So what: Centralized design bureaus kept high-value design work in Russia, preserving lock-in.
“You end up with just like one kind of clone factory that is also spitting out one specific design.” ▶ 1:08:21
CANDU is fully indigenously Canadian, supported by Chalk River/CNL, and offers importers a 'third option' beyond Russia, China and the US, with more independence because it needs fewer heavy forgings and its fuel supply is easier to localize.
thesis🗣 Michael Seely (Atomic Blender)conviction: mediumCanadaCANDUChalk RiverCNL
So what: CANDU's localization advantages could win non-aligned buyers.
“The fuel supply is a little bit easier to localize” ▶ 1:05:36

Real Vision · Raoul Pal 1 episode(s) · 38 insights

The Next Global Power Shift Has Begun with Dr. Pippa Malmgren53 min38 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Geopolitical analyst Michael Rosenwald interviews economist Pippa Malmgren, who argues the real AI fight is builders vs bureaucrats (control vs chaos), the Arctic and space are the new strategic frontiers, and a US-China-Russia deal (Shenzhen meeting) on Ukraine is imminent — a deal that would spark an Eastern Europe reconstruction boom while exposing Western Europe's balance sheet via Ukraine loans and Euroclear-held Russian assets. They also cover Iran/Strait of Hormuz, Taiwan, and a post-midterms Trump focused on foreign policy wins.
AI: builders vs bureaucrats, not US vs China · 7Arctic: data centers, food and polar space infrastructure · 5Space as the new commanding heights; Europe lagging · 4Ukraine peace deal and Western Europe's balance-sheet risk · 6Russia-China-US realignment and Taiwan · 8US politics, Iran and the Strait of Hormuz · 8
AI: builders vs bureaucrats, not US vs China 7
Pippa Malmgren argues the AI contest is not a race between nations (US vs China) but between 'builders and bureaucrats' / 'control versus chaos' factions that exist across and within national borders, since breakthroughs by either side's engineers are quickly replicated.
framework🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highAIChinaUnited States
So what: Analyze AI policy by faction (open/build vs control) rather than by country.
“I think the real battle is between maybe we can categorize them as builders and bureaucrats or control versus chaos.” ▶ 4:40
The UAE has made AI a cabinet member, and Malmgren says it intends to use AI to run clinical-trial simulations and produce drugs locally rather than wait on the US FDA process, leveraging proximity (within a 5-hour flight) to East Africa, Central Asia, Western India and the Middle East gene pools.
thesis🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highUnited Arab EmiratesFDAAI
So what: UAE could become a hub for cutting-edge, AI-accelerated drug development outside US regulation.
“we're going to have AI as a cabinet member in our government, which it already is.” ▶ 6:48
Malmgren contends that FDA-style clinical trials take years and rely on a narrow test population (she says white males), which the UAE sees as an unsuitable basis for medicines for its region.
contrarian🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: mediumFDAUnited Arab Emirates
So what: Regulatory arbitrage in drug trials is a structural threat to incumbent pharma economics.
If AI-driven drug development like the UAE's succeeds, Malmgren says American pharmaceutical companies could go out of business and doctors would lose their monopoly on treatment decisions.
prediction🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: mediumAmerican pharmaceutical industryUnited Arab Emirates
So what: Long-term disruption risk for US pharma and healthcare incumbents.
“That means American pharmaceutical companies are going to go out of business.” ▶ 7:51
Malmgren sides with Scott Bessent and Elon Musk in the view that the only way to outrun the sovereign debt problem is to lean into automation, AI and robotics as fast as possible.
thesis🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highScott BessentElon MuskAIrobotics
So what: Policy bias in the US Treasury favors accelerating AI/automation as a debt solution.
“the only way we can outrun the debt problem and address it and solve it is by leaning into automation, AI, robotics as fast as possible.” ▶ 9:42
Malmgren claims South Korea and Sweden have the fastest adoption rates of automation/AI/robotics and are producing jobs, work and income faster than anywhere else, supporting an 'augment, not replace' framing.
data_point🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: mediumSouth KoreaSweden
So what: Counter-evidence to AI job-loss fears; high-adoption economies may outperform.
“The two countries with the fastest adoption rates of these things are South Korea and Sweden.” ▶ 9:57
Citing the Jacquard loom (1830), washing machines, calculators and cars, Malmgren argues automation has historically produced more jobs and higher living standards, and AI fear is mainly a framing problem.
framework🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highJacquard weaving loom
So what: Expect adaptation rather than mass unemployment; fear-driven policy is the risk.
“we have more accountants now than we've ever had around the world.” ▶ 11:11
Arctic: data centers, food and polar space infrastructure 5
Iceland aims to be 'the Switzerland of data centers', using geothermal and hydro power, abundant water and cold climate for cooling, with modern silent facilities; Malmgren says Greenland has the same advantages.
thesis🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highIcelandGreenlanddata centers
So what: Arctic is an emerging low-cost, sustainable compute location.
“Iceland has said we want to be the Switzerland of data centers.” ▶ 12:42
Waste heat from Icelandic data centers is used to heat greenhouses, and Iceland is starting to produce tomatoes and bananas at scale, leading Malmgren to suggest the Arctic could become central to global food production.
causal🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: mediumIcelanddata centers
So what: Second-order: data-center heat reuse enables Arctic agriculture.
“already Iceland starting to produce tomatoes and bananas at scale” ▶ 13:45
Malmgren reports leaders of Iceland and Greenland told her they don't want jobs but want to be 'like Saudi Arabia' — letting others use automation on their resources while they get paid — and predicts such small countries will leapfrog big ones.
anecdote🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: mediumIcelandGreenlandSaudi Arabia
So what: Small resource-rich states may capture AI-era rents with minimal labor.
“These little countries are going to leapfrog into the future faster than the big ones” ▶ 15:07
Michael Rosenwald is skeptical of Greenland minerals/rare-earths exploitation due to poor infrastructure and lack of labor force, but sees data centers as a more viable proposition there.
contrarian🗣 Michael Rosenwaldconviction: mediumGreenlandrare earthsdata centers
So what: Favor Greenland compute plays over mining plays.
A major reason for US interest in Greenland and the Arctic is the space race: polar satellites need polar ground stations for the lowest-latency data transmission.
causal🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highGreenlandArcticsatellites
So what: Arctic ground infrastructure is strategic for space/defense.
“you need polar satellites that connect to polar ground stations for the lowest latency data transmission.” ▶ 17:26
Space as the new commanding heights; Europe lagging 4
Malmgren says space is the new commanding heights of warfare: tanks in Ukraine are useless without satellite guidance, illustrated by the Pentagon turning down Ukraine's bandwidth when it was not complying in negotiations.
thesis🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: highUkrainePentagonsatellites
So what: Mega-constellation owners hold decisive military leverage.
“space is the new commanding heights for war” ▶ 17:38
Malmgren criticizes Germany's plan to convert automotive lines to tank production, saying every tank is useless without a mega-constellation of satellites, which Europe hasn't thought through.
contrarian🗣 Pippa Malmgrenconviction: mediumGermanssatellites
So what: European defense spending on legacy hardware may be misallocated.

Odd Lots · Bloomberg 1 episode(s) · 37 insights

How an American AI Company Uses Chinese Open-Weight Models | Odd Lots49 min37 insights2026-11-20
▶ Watch on YouTube
Minerva co-founder Daniel Saedi (ex-Bridgewater) explains how a US AI marketing startup uses frontier closed models to build product but fine-tunes Chinese open-weight models like Qwen and Kimi for cheap, repeatable production tasks, optimizing cost per completed task; the discussion covers fine-tuning economics (Harvey's ~$6M Tenant model, $7-9/M-token fine-tuning at Fireworks), data as the real moat, distillation debates, abliterated-model cyber risk, and a heuristic that the US is massively short compute (5-10M Blackwells vs ~100M knowledge workers).
Frontier-for-building, open-weight-for-serving economics · 9Fine-tuning open weights as app-layer moat · 8Commoditized inference/fine-tuning infrastructure · 4Data is the alpha · 5Chinese open weights: distillation and the catch-up debate · 3Obliterated models and cyber risk · 3Long-run compute shortage · 5
Frontier-for-building, open-weight-for-serving economics 9
Minerva uses frontier closed models (Anthropic's Claude, OpenAI's models and others) to build its product because developer time is expensive, but for repeatable production tasks it optimizes cost per completed task rather than cost per million tokens.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highMinervaAnthropicOpenAI
So what: App-layer demand for frontier models skews to coding/building; production inference is price-sensitive and migrates to cheaper models.
“you care about cost per completed task, which is a lot different than, you know, looking in the media where everyone cares about cost per million tokens” ▶ 6:33
For an AI application company, margin is everything above the minimum acceptable task completion achieved at the lowest cost, so the goal is the cheapest model that clears the customer's quality bar.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highMinerva
So what: Model choice at the app layer is a margin decision, not a capability race, which pressures premium token pricing.
“how do I make sure that I get the minimum acceptable task completion for the lowest amount of costs? Because everything outside of that is our margin” ▶ 6:55
Saedi ranks the benefits of open-weight models for US companies as: (1) cost, (2) data sovereignty/control, and (3) competitive leverage against single vendors.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Cost is the primary driver of open-weight adoption; sovereignty and vendor leverage are secondary but growing.
“I think the most important thing is cost” ▶ 13:08
The build-vs-buy equation is: per-token lab cost times number of tasks must exceed the upfront fine-tuning cost plus monthly operating cost of hosting the model for open weights to win, and it is true in many but not all cases.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: high
So what: Expect app-layer switching to be use-case-by-use-case rather than wholesale.
Harvey, which charges a fixed per-seat price, moved first to fine-tune its own legal model ('Tenant') because rising usage drove variable costs up against fixed revenue; usage-priced legal-tech rival 'Lagos' (as transcribed) is now exploring fine-tuning in response.
causal🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highHarveyLagos
So what: Seat-priced AI apps have the strongest incentive to defect from frontier APIs to protect operating margins.
“Harvey has a per seat cost which is fixed and Lagos charges usage” ▶ 35:36
Joe Weisenthal frames the app-layer business model as selling a service at a recurring price while token prices fall ~90% per year, capturing the widening margin; Saedi confirms that is how Minerva and peers think about it.
thesis🗣 Joe Weisenthal / Daniel Saediconviction: highMinerva
So what: Token price deflation accrues to application companies with fixed pricing rather than to model vendors.
“that's exactly how I think us and other companies are thinking about it” ▶ 35:16
Minerva's token budget has risen, not fallen, since the team moved fully to agentic coding around Opus 4.5 in October of last year, but it avoids 'token maxing' of $10-20K per engineer and aims for outcome-maxing.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumMinervaopus 4.5
So what: Even cost-conscious startups are increasing total AI spend as models improve; aggregate token demand keeps rising.
“we're not spending like ten, 15, $20,000 per engineer for no token maxing” ▶ 37:55
Even if open models win production workloads, many companies are still paying an 'Anthropic tax' or 'OpenAI tax', and public claims of using Chinese models (e.g. Coinbase's Brian Armstrong citing Kimi) can be posturing to gain pricing leverage.
contrarian🗣 Joe Weisenthalconviction: mediumCoinbaseBrian ArmstrongAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Headlines about firms switching to open models may overstate actual frontier-lab revenue loss.
“I think a lot of companies are paying an ant tax or an open AI tax” ▶ 44:38
Tracy Alloway notes that privately, many large US companies admit to using Chinese models like Qwen, but not in public.
anecdote🗣 Tracy Allowayconviction: mediumQuinn
So what: Chinese open-weight adoption in US enterprise is likely underreported.
“A lot of them we'll talk about like Quinn and. Yeah, but not in public” ▶ 43:50
Fine-tuning open weights as app-layer moat 8
Saedi argues fine-tuning open-weight models is the primary benefit to the application layer today, letting startups without the labs' billions in data specialize a general model on customer data.
thesis🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highMinerva
So what: App companies with proprietary data can build differentiated, cheaper models, eroding frontier-lab pricing power on specific tasks.
“this is the primary benefit to the application layer right now is the ability to fine tune on top of those openings” ▶ 9:48
Minerva fine-tuned Qwen on a few thousand graded examples of complex audience queries (natural language to SQL), producing a model faster than closed models and better for its use case; out-of-the-box Qwen roughly matched an OpenAI model but trailed top frontier models.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highMinervaQuinnOpenAI
So what: A few thousand labeled examples can be enough to beat frontier APIs on narrow tasks.
“we have a few thousand of these labeled examples where you have a very complicated query” ▶ 11:27
Application companies like Harvey and Cognition are fine-tuning Kimi on their proprietary data to match or beat frontier performance on customer tasks at lower cost, creating an advantage they can charge margin on.
thesis🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumHarveycognitionKimmie
So what: Leading AI apps are building on Chinese open weights, a structural channel of Chinese model influence in US software.
Harvey reportedly spent about $6 million in compute to reach the frontier on legal tasks with its fine-tuned model, which Saedi says is 2-3 orders of magnitude off what frontier-model training/running costs.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumHarvey
So what: Domain-frontier performance is attainable for single-digit millions, a fraction of lab capex.
“Harvey on their tenant model, spent about $6 million in compute” ▶ 19:19
Fine-tunes do not transfer across model versions (e.g. Kimi 3 to Kimi 3.1); only the training dataset carries over, and because training recipes are undisclosed, re-convergence is uncertain, creating ongoing operational cost and risk.
causal🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highKimi
So what: Fine-tuning carries a recurring maintenance cost that must be priced into the build-vs-buy decision.
“It doesn't simply transfer over” ▶ 15:45
Open-weight competition gives smaller companies leverage to force the frontier labs into value-based pricing, since the alternative is no longer 'no automation' but a decent homegrown model.
causal🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Open-weight models cap frontier-lab pricing power with enterprise and app customers.
“it gives the, the leverage back to, to the smaller companies” ▶ 15:11
Saedi expects data sovereignty to be a huge issue in 2027 as companies realize they send data to Anthropic and OpenAI without the model improving for their use case, a point he attributes to Alex Karp and Satya Nadella.
prediction🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumKarpSatyaAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Rising sovereignty concern favors fine-tunable open weights and enterprise self-hosting.
“I imagine this is going to be a huge issue in 27” ▶ 13:31
Open source and open weight are distinct: Chinese labs release model weights (the end artifact) for self-hosting, but not the training code, data and recipe.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: high
So what: Open-weight users remain dependent on opaque upstream training choices.
Commoditized inference/fine-tuning infrastructure 4
Fine-tuning and hosting have been commoditized over the past year by providers like Fireworks, Modal and Thinking Machines; previously it required a team of inference engineers, which is why few beyond research groups and large firms like Uber attempted it despite Llama being open for two years.
causal🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highfireworksmodalthinking machinesUberllama
So what: Lower barriers expand the addressable base of open-weight adopters.
Fireworks charges roughly $7-9 per million input and output tokens for fine-tuning, and some well-performing fine-tunes have cost as little as $90 for the final training run.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highfireworks
So what: Fine-tuning cost has collapsed to near-trivial levels; data quality is the binding constraint.
“I'm going to pay around 7 to $9 for per million input and million output tokens” ▶ 18:54
Saedi says Fireworks and Modal are each at about $1 billion in annual revenue run rate with likely good unit economics, showing commoditization of a layer need not drive margins to zero.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumfireworksModo
So what: Inference/fine-tuning platforms are a sizable, profitable layer benefiting from open-weight adoption.
“both of them are sitting at $1 billion in error” ▶ 20:45
DeepSeek recently published its entire inference framework, which Fireworks, Modal and others use to improve serving efficiency; Chinese labs also pioneered techniques like sparse models that save compute.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumdeep seafireworksmodal
So what: Chinese open research lowers serving costs industry-wide, pressuring inference pricing.
Data is the alpha 5
What separates a good fine-tune from a bad one is the data and evals, which is why labs pay data firms (transcribed as 'McCaw' and 'Serge') billions; the 'alpha' is in the data.
thesis🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highMcCawSerge
So what: Expert data/eval vendors capture value as compute and fine-tuning commoditize.
“the alpha per se is, as you would say, is in the data right now” ▶ 21:15
Micro1, which Saedi calls the most legitimate company-data procurer, has paid $250-500K per complete company dataset and $50-100K just for referrals.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumMicro One
So what: Corporate process data is a newly monetizable asset with steep market prices.
“they're spending anywhere from 250 to 500 K per complete data set from a company” ▶ 24:00
AI needs process data ('show your work'), not just outcomes: in construction, blueprints are available but the architect-contractor-client comments explaining changes are scarce, whereas marketing data (ads, call logs, website activity) is plentiful from the programmatic era.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: high
So what: Verticals with abundant process data (marketing) are automatable sooner than data-poor ones (construction).
Marketing data can be built into RL environments that simulate customer acquisition, e.g. testing whether an ad gets an AI agent to click through and buy.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: medium
So what: RL environments are becoming the unit of training data products.
Joe Weisenthal cites Google buying Spirit Airlines for $10 million for its corporate email and calendar data, and wonders whether zombie firms are worth more liquidated as training data.
contrarian🗣 Joe Weisenthalconviction: exploratoryGoogleSpirit Airlines
So what: Corporate data may create hidden asset value in distressed firms.
“You got to wonder, how many zombie firms are out there that would be worth more if they just liquidated and sold all of their emails and corporate data to a lab?” ▶ 24:31
Chinese open weights: distillation and the catch-up debate 3
Reflection AI, the leading US open-weight lab (allegedly being bought by Nvidia), trained its flagship on a 12-14K Blackwell cluster yet landed 3-6 months behind Chinese open models, which were themselves ~3 months behind the frontier; Saedi infers distillation is not the main reason Chinese labs are ahead.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumreflection AINvidia
So what: Chinese labs' competitiveness reflects genuine research, so their open weights will likely keep pressuring US pricing.
“it was probably about 3 to 6 months behind the Chinese open source models” ▶ 30:01
Joe Weisenthal pushes back that open-weight value may be subsidized by Chinese terms-of-service violations (distillation) that an American lab like the one behind Llama would not commit; Saedi agrees distillation is real and helps, citing the Kimi-Astra performance similarity, but says its share is debatable.
causal🗣 Joe Weisenthal / Daniel Saediconviction: mediumKimiAstra
So what: Regulatory or technical crackdowns on distillation are a risk to the open-weight cost advantage.
“distillation is real, distillation is is real” ▶ 31:09
Benchmarks are becoming meaningless; the real signal of Chinese models catching up is whether practitioners can swap Kimi in as their daily driver for coding and production workflows without degradation.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highKimi
So what: Watch real-world substitution, not leaderboard scores, to judge commoditization of frontier intelligence.
“the benchmarks are starting to become meaningless” ▶ 33:52
Obliterated models and cyber risk 3
The biggest risk of open-weight frontier models is 'abliteration': disabling refusal neurons in models like DeepSeek, yielding cheap uncensored models that can be used for hacking; online theory attributes current hacks to swarms of such models.
thesis🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highdeep seek
So what: Cyber-risk is the most likely trigger for open-weight regulation (e.g. KYC for weight downloads), a key policy risk for the app layer.
“people are running swarms of these deep seek obliterated models” ▶ 26:13
An abliterated DeepSeek model costs about $0.30 per million blended tokens, roughly 10x cheaper than Sonnet, which is itself about 10x cheaper than the top frontier model, so roughly 1/100th the cost.
data_point🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: mediumdeep seasonnet
So what: Near-frontier offensive capability is available at near-zero marginal cost.
“It's about $0.30 per million or blended” ▶ 25:54
Saedi says the labs' real concern with Chinese open weights is not data exfiltration to China but misuse via modified weights, and he suggests a middle ground such as KYC before downloading weights while preserving startup access.
prediction🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: exploratory
So what: Policy outcomes on open weights directly affect app-layer cost structures.
“you have to KYC before you can download the weights” ▶ 27:01
Long-run compute shortage 5
Saedi's first-principles heuristic: a fully AI-native knowledge worker needs about one GPU's worth of token consumption per year; with 5-10 million Blackwells deployed in the US versus ~100 million knowledge workers, the economy is massively short compute.
framework🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: highBlackwell
So what: Supports a long-run bullish compute-demand thesis despite short-term overbuild narratives.
“we're massively short of that need in the future” ▶ 0:38
Saedi expects that roughly four years out there will be about one B300 worth of compute per knowledge worker, and that temporary overbuild narratives are noise around a long-term shortage.
prediction🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: medium
So what: Near-term GPU glut fears may be a buying opportunity for compute exposure.
“in the long term we're still massively short the amount of compute we need” ▶ 41:33
Joe Weisenthal notes H100 rental price indices (Silicon Data) are still rising even amid AI-trade volatility, while Saedi reports compute for small fine-tuning jobs is readily available, unlike two years ago when H100s were unavailable.
data_point🗣 Joe Weisenthal / Daniel Saediconviction: mediumsilicon data
So what: Spot GPU pricing remains firm; scarcity concentrates at large-scale training rather than small fine-tunes.
Reports that Greg Brockman asked Elon Musk for compute while waiting for White House medals suggest frontier labs remain fundamentally short compute.
anecdote🗣 Daniel Saedi (Minerva co-founder)conviction: lowGreg BrockmanElon
So what: Lab-level compute hunger persists.
“Greg Brockman asked Elon for compute” ▶ 41:56
Joe Weisenthal cites an MIT research report where a frontier model (transcribed as 'Astor'/'after six') was fed Qwen's weights and directly edited them to improve math (fixing a bug), hinting at models improving models without traditional training.
anecdote🗣 Joe Weisenthalconviction: exploratoryMITAstor
So what: If models can edit weights directly, the highest-value use of frontier compute may be AI R&D itself.

The Prof G Show · Scott Galloway 1 episode(s) · 37 insights

Robert Reich: Why Billionaires Borrow Instead of Paying Taxes56 min37 insights2026-11-19
▶ Watch on YouTube
Scott Galloway interviews former Labor Secretary Robert Reich (auto-captioned as 'Robert Rich') after a Democratic midterm wave: Reich argues the economy is dangerously concentrated in five or six companies (Nvidia's leadership crisis and an alleged AI pipeline to China as Exhibit A, with too-big-to-fail bailout risk if AI busts), calls for Big Tech breakups plus antitrust against banks, oil, ag/seed and ISPs, and explains how billionaires borrow against wealth to avoid taxable income, favoring a wealth tax or ending step-up basis while Galloway argues wealth taxes fail. Also covers 2028 candidates, union decline, minimum wage and higher-ed costs.

AI concentration, Nvidia shock and too-big-to-fail risk 6
Robert Reich argues that having a large chunk of the US economy dependent on one person (whether Elon Musk or the head of Nvidia) is a recipe for potential disaster, likening it to building an economy around robber barons as in the Gilded Age.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highNvidiaElon Musk
So what: Key-person risk in AI megacaps is a systemic, not just idiosyncratic, risk.
“you can't build an economy around you know a handful of robber barons uh as we did in the last guilded age” ▶ 24:54
Reich says the resignation of Nvidia's leader is not only a big problem for Nvidia but for AI in general, because the AI buildout depended heavily on that company and that individual.
causal🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highNvidiaAI
So what: Leadership disruption at Nvidia can transmit to the whole AI trade.
“it's a big problem for AI in general because AI really did depend uh upon uh his company and him” ▶ 25:12
Reich and Galloway frame the Nvidia situation as a national security failure: a direct pipeline of AI capability to China was allegedly established, Marco Rubio says he alerted Trump in May of last year, and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick reportedly also knew, yet nothing was done.
data_point🗣 Robert Reichconviction: mediumNvidiaChinaMarco RubioHoward LutnickDonald Trump
So what: Expect congressional investigations and tighter AI export-control scrutiny, a regulatory overhang for Nvidia.
“Marco Rubio said that he alerted Trump uh, to this problem [clears throat] uh, in May of last year and nothing was done.” ▶ 26:04
Reich urges congressional Democrats to push the Nvidia/China national security breach 'very very hard', signaling it will become a political oversight issue.
prediction🗣 Robert Reichconviction: mediumNvidiaDemocratsCongress
So what: Headline risk for Nvidia and AI chip exporters from hearings and investigations.
“I think the um the Democrats in Congress have got to push this very very hard.” ▶ 25:31
Reich warns that if AI goes down because Nvidia or another big AI company goes bust, there will be demands for bailouts, as in the 2008 financial crisis and the dot-com bubble, because companies have become too big to fail.
prediction🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highNvidiaAI2008 financial crisisdot-com bubble
So what: An AI bust could trigger a policy bailout debate; concentration implies socialized downside.
“we can't let companies get so big that they are too big to fail. And that's what's happened in America.” ▶ 30:00
Reich says there is no reason for the US economy to be so centralized on basically five or six companies and calls this too dangerous for the economy.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highBig Tech
So what: Mega-cap index concentration is viewed by policy figures as a systemic danger warranting breakups.
“there's no reason to have an economy that is so centralized on basically five or six companies. It's too dangerous for the economy.” ▶ 29:13
Antitrust revival and breakup targets 8
Reich identifies Robert Bork's book The Antitrust Paradox as the single intellectual cause of the end of antitrust enforcement since the 1980s, by persuading the Reagan administration and judges to judge mergers solely on consumer welfare rather than political power.
causal🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highRobert BorkThe Antitrust ParadoxReagan administration
So what: The consumer-welfare standard is the doctrinal pillar a Democratic antitrust push would target.
“it's rare that you get one individual thinker and one book that changes the public policies in an entire area of the economy” ▶ 27:39
Reich says the original Sherman Act (1890) and Clayton Act (1914) were focused on preventing companies from gaining political power to influence legislation, and that antitrust should return to that standard; Galloway calls for a return to a Brandeisian test of market power.
framework🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highSherman ActClayton ActBrandeis
So what: A political-power standard would put far more large companies in scope than consumer-welfare analysis.
Reich advocates both blocking large companies from further acquisitions and actively breaking up existing concentrations of capital, starting with Big Tech.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highBig Tech
So what: Big Tech M&A and structural breakup risk would rise under Democratic control.
“it's also important to start busting up big concentrations of capital. Uh, and I would start with high-tech with big tech.” ▶ 29:02
Reich would have Democrats pass a new antitrust/anti-monopoly law because he considers the Sherman Act and Clayton Act inadequate to today's monopoly problems.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: mediumSherman ActClayton ActDemocrats
So what: Legislative, not just enforcement, risk for concentrated industries.
Beyond tech, Reich says antitrust enforcers should target big banking, agriculture (food processing and highly concentrated seed corn), big oil, and major internet providers.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highbig bankingbig oilfood processingseed corninternet providers
So what: Banks, oil majors, ag/seed oligopolies and ISPs are the named next-wave antitrust targets.
“I would say uh big banking.” ▶ 47:59
Reich argues that once an industry is down to two or three companies, as in big oil where he says there are now four companies with two US giants, they have ample opportunity to coordinate prices and investments.
framework🗣 Robert Reichconviction: mediumbig oil
So what: Oligopoly structure itself is treated as evidence of pricing power warranting enforcement.
“when you get down to two or three companies, they are clearly uh they have all kinds of opportunities to to coordinate their prices and their investments”
Reich says major internet providers exhibit extraordinary concentration and coordination of prices and deserve antitrust examination.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: mediuminternet providers
So what: Broadband/ISP incumbents face potential pricing scrutiny.
Galloway argues the FTC and DOJ have been in a slumber on antitrust for 30 years, with regulatory capture allowing concentrated corporations to extract more rents from consumers and labor, and floats Roosevelt-style trust busting across big tech, big beef, big pharma and big ag.
thesis🗣 Scott Gallowayconviction: highFTCDOJbig pharmabig tech
So what: Concentration-driven margins are framed as rent extraction that policy could unwind.
Taxes on the wealthy: borrowing, step-up basis and wealth taxes 6
Reich explains that the very wealthy (hundreds of millions to billions) do not depend on income; they borrow against their wealth for living expenses, leaving little taxable income, which is why many billionaires pay almost nothing.
causal🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highbillionairescapital gains
So what: Buy-borrow-die is the key target of wealth tax policy; securities-based lending is central to it.
“they borrow against their wealth for their living expenses. So they don't have any taxable income or very very little taxable income which explains why so many billionaires don't pay a thing” ▶ 49:39
Reich proposes ending the stepped-up basis at death rule as an alternative to a wealth tax, since it currently lets heirs sell appreciated assets with no capital gains tax, so entire fortunes pass across generations never taxed.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highstepped up basis at deathcapital gains
So what: Ending step-up would change estate planning and could alter holding behavior of appreciated assets.
“there are entire fortunes that can pass generation to generation and do pass generation to generation uh and uh and are never taxed” ▶ 50:45
Galloway disagrees with Reich on wealth taxes, arguing capital is mobile and that 13 of the 16 European nations that implemented wealth taxes repealed them; he prefers cutting the estate exemption from $30 million to $1 million, more progressive taxes and an alternative minimum tax.
contrarian🗣 Scott Gallowayconviction: highwealth taxEuropeestate exemptionalternative minimum tax
So what: Even among progressives, estate-tax and AMT changes look more viable than a wealth tax.
“13 of the 16 nations in Europe that implemented them and ended up repealing them” ▶ 51:08
Reich counters that the US already has wealth taxes in the form of property taxes, but property, capital gains and estate taxes are hollowed out by loopholes created by Washington's large tax attorney and lobbying industry, so any reform must be simple.
framework🗣 Robert Reichconviction: mediumproperty taxescapital gainsestate tax
So what: Complexity is the channel through which tax increases get neutralized; simple designs are more durable.
“what you need to have is a simple tax regime” ▶ 52:18
California's Proposition 40 wealth tax failed, which Reich attributes to California billionaires spending hundreds of millions of dollars to oppose it with a flood of ads warning 'they'll come for you next'.
data_point🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highProposition 40Californiabillionaires
So what: State-level wealth taxes face heavy funded opposition; Prop 40 failure reduces near-term California wealth-tax risk.
“the billionaires in California uh put hundreds of millions of dollars into into opposing it”
Reich would remove the cap on income subject to Social Security tax, calling payroll taxes one of the highest taxes most people pay, while raising taxes on the wealthy at least to pre-Trump levels to fund health care and child care.
thesis🗣 Robert Reichconviction: highSocial SecurityObamacareMedicaid
So what: Higher-earner payroll tax and top-rate increases are on the Democratic agenda for 2028.
“There is no reason that there should be a cap on income subject to social security.” ▶ 6:48
Post-midterm politics and 2028 outlook 8
Reich reads the midterm 'blue tsunami' as a repudiation of Trump and a demand for help with kitchen-table costs (groceries, gas, health care), with independents (45% of Americans) swinging heavily to Democrats, but says no one knows whether it is a fundamental realignment.

Labor power, wages and health care 6

Reich cites private-sector unionization falling from one-third of workers in the 1950s to about 6% today, attributing much of the decline to corporate union-busting since the 1980s and weak penalties (firing an organizer only costs back pay and reinstatement).

Higher education costs and vocational paths 3
